1012. ELEAZER MYERS was indicted for stealing' on the 13th of February, 2 violins, value 3l. 10s., and 1 violin bow, value 3s., the goods of Thomas King.
THOMAS KING. I keep a broker's shop in Upper Rathbone-place. On the 13th of February the prisoner came to my shop; he said he belonged to a Portuguese vessel - he took a violin out of my window, and asked the price; I told him 2l.; I then showed him another violin and a bow - he asked the price of that; I said 30s.; he then said he belonged to a Portuguese vessel, the Three Sisters, lying in St. Katharine Dock, and was employed by the captain to look out for violias for him; he asked if I would have any objection to let him take the violins to show the captain, if he left above the value of them - I said I had no objection to his taking them, if he left, the full value, if he would bring back the money or the violins, as I would take no goods or any thing of that sort in exchange; I particularly told him that; he then said he would wish to have the property he would leave back again, as it was of more value to him than the violins - he then took out these two shawls from a paper he had under his arm; he knelt down on his right knee, lifted up his two hands, and said, "Pounds more as more as, by Jesus Christ me will not deceive you;" I understood by that 10l.; I then let him have the violins, which were worth 3l. 10s., and he promised to bring back them or the money by three o'clock - he did not return; I did not see him again till the 24th of February, when I met him by accident in Tottenham-court-road; I stopped him, and asked where the violins were - he said he had got them, and I should be sure to have them again; that he had been looking for my shop two or three days, and could not find it - he told me he would give me the violins; he led me on to Cheapside, and opposite Bow church I gave him in charge.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Did he speak tolerably good English? A. He spoke broken English - I did not bargain with him for a shawl for my wife in lien of one of the violins; I did not take the shawls for the violins - he left them as security; here are the shawls.
COURT. Q. You had not sold the violins? A. No, my Lord, not without he brought back the money; I gave him to an officer - he ran from him he was pursued, and taken.
JOHN GREEN. I am an officer. I took the prisoner in Cox-square when he ran away from the other Policeman.
THOMAS MORFORD. I am the Police-constable. The prisoner got from me, but was not out of my sight; I pursued him about a quarter of a mile - I was knocked down in Cox-square; I was kicked, beaten, and very ill-treated- I nearly lost one of my fingers, and could not go on duty for three days; we pushed the prisoner into a shop, and sent for assistance - several officers came to us; there were hundreds of people round us.
SAMUEL STEVENS. I am foreman to a pawnbroker. I have the two violins, which were pawned on the 13th of February.(Property produced and sworn to.)
MR. MANNING. These shawls are worth about 4s. each.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to the prosecutor's shop, and asked if he wanted to buy shawls for his wife - he said Yes - he asked how much, and said he had no money but had good violins; I being a musician, can play a violin- he asked to change shawls for violins - I do not know whether I have a right to change.
GUILTY. Aged 27. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-210]
1013. JOHN NEWBERRY was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of March, 1 mahogany book-rest, value 5s., the goods of William Artand and another.
WILLIAM ARTAND JUN. I live with my father - his name is William; he has one partner - they are in the book trade, and live in Queen Anne-street. On the 2nd of March I was at work at an opposite house, and saw the prisoner bring a book-rest out of my father's shop and give it to a butcher-boy, who was waiting outside - I pursued, and had him taken.
STEPHEN ARTAND. There was a book-rest lost from our shop, but I did not see it; my nephew, the witness, told me of it - I ran to Wigmore-street; I saw the prisoner, and had him taken - I saw a butcher-boy, but he got away.
GUILTY. Aged 13.
Recommended to Mercy. - Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18320405-211]
1014. GABRIEL MOSS was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of March, 1 watch, value 3l.; 1 watch-chain, value 1s., and 1 watch-key, value 1s., the goods of John Clowsley, from his person.
THOMAS BARKER. I am an iron-founder. I was in Finsbury-square on the 21st of March; there was a great crowd, and among them I saw the prisoner - he put a watch into his pocket, and ran away with another person; I said to a witness, "Do not say any thing now, for fearwe should get hurt;" there were six more besides the prisoner; he ran down some streets, and when he had got out of the party I called Stop thief! I saw him take the watch from his pocket, and throw it down.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Where do you live? A. In Field-place, Battle-bridge; I work in Old Gravel-lane - I may have been in Rosemary-lane, but not to my knowledge; I went to Finsbury-square the same as other people to see what was doing - I did not expect any thing to occur; I was going to see my sister at Hackney.
JOHN CLOWSLEY. I am a carpenter. I was in Finsbury-square on the 21st of March - I was pressed against several persons, and the prisoner, who was one, put his hand up against me, and in an instant my watch was gone- I caught one who was as near me as the prisoner - I held him as long as I could, and called for assistance, but I got two or three violent blows, and was forced to let him go.
Cross-examined. Q. There was a great crowd? A. Yes, and they closed about me - the prisoner shuffled me about; I do not know who pushed him - I do not say he took my watch; I think I should know the features of the man I seized - it was not the prisoner.
CHARLES ELLIS. I am a well-digger. I was in the square, and saw the prosecutor with a crowd about him; I saw the prisoner take the watch from his pocket.
Cross-examined. Q. What did the prisoner do? A. He put one of his arms up to his throat, and took the watch with his other hand - the prosecutor caught hold of another man; I have worked for the master of St. George's workhouse - I was at the Mansion-house; I did not then say I had been out of employ three months; I had been in employ, but did not work that day, because it was a holiday - I went to see the victuals given away, but there was none; I was was close to the prisoner - he trod on my foes; I did not seize him, for fear of my own head - he had not done any thing to me, but I saw him attempt several persons; I did not see an officer at the time near me, or I should have told him of it.
JOSEPH STONE. I am an officer. I have the watch - the prisoner was given into my custody in Bishopsgate-street.(Property produced and sworn to.)
THOMAS PARKER. He threw it down on the curb-stone.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-212]
OLD COURT. WEDNESDAY, APRIL 11.
Second Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1015. ELEANOR GUNN was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 22nd of March, 1 pair of trousers, value 10s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 2s.; 1 shirt, value 3s.; 1 jacket, value 1s., and 1 pinafore, value 3d., the goods of William Ladd. well knowing them to have been stolen.
WILLIAM LADD. I live in Lower Chapman-street, Cannon-street-road. On the 22nd of March my son William left my house; in consequence of something which passed between him and me, when I saw him, I went with an officer to a house in White's-yard - the prisoner was at the door of the house; I went in and told her I had come for some things she had of mine - I mentioned a pinafore, the fellow to one the boy had on; I told her she had received stolen goods from my son, and read her an inventory, and mentioned trousers, a hat, stockings, and jacket; she produced them according to my account - they were worth about 16s.; she said she gave my son 16d. for them, and that he said he could have brought a great many more things if he liked; they are here, and are his clothes, but have been cut and disfigured - the collar of the jacket has been taken off and altered; I missed them from my son's box the day he left the house.
WILLIAM LADD. I ran away from my father's on the 22nd of March - I took my clothes out of my box, put them in my pinafore, and went round to White's-yard, which is about a quarter of an hour's walk; I had been nearly three years in the Refuge for the Destitute, and when I came home I ran away directly, and sold my clothes to the prisoner; she asked me if I could get her any thing to sell - I told her Yes; she then said, "Come in, come in, and I will reckon with you;" she then looked at the things, and asked how much I wanted for them - I said 1s. for the trousers - she said, "No, I will give you 6d.:" she reckoned up all the things, and gave me 16d. - she knew who I was, because I had been there before.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not ask how you came by them, and were you not asking people in the street to buy them? A. No; I had them in my pinafore - she asked if I had any things to sell; she had told me a week before if I got any thing she would buy them of me.
WILLIAM GRANT (Police-constable H 37.) I accompanied the prosecutor to the prisoner's house - she stood at the door; the boy said, "That is the woman who bought the clothes;" I said, "Do you know this boy?" she said No; I said, "You never saw him before!" she said No - the prosecutor said, "Yes, you have been the rum of my child - you have his clothes, and must produce them;" she said, "Well, walk in," and produced them from a box - she said she gave him 16d. for them.
WILLIAM LADD. They were in very good condition when they were taken; they have since been cut and hacked about, and worn; my son is not quite thirteen years old - I lost them on the 22nd, and found them on the 26th.
Prisoner's Defence. He was asking people in the street to buy the clothes, and having four children I thought I might as well buy them as any body else - he sold a pair of trousers and stockings before I saw him.
WILLIAM LADD, JUN. I did not sell them; I gave them to a boy who I knew.
GUILTY. Aged 40. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-213]
1016. THOMAS PURFLEET was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of September, 1 tea-kettle, value 2s., the goods of Thomas Capps.
THOMAS CAPPS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Old-street-road. On the 24th of September I lost a tea-kettle, which the officer produced the same evening; I cannot say at what the officer produced the same evening; I cannot say at what time it was taken.
GEORGE KEMP (Police-constable, N 82.) On the 24th of September, about ten o'clock at night, I was in Boot-street, and saw the prisoner in company with severalnotorious thieves, with something under his jacket - he gave it to another boy, and made off; I stopped the boy, and took it from him - it was this kettle; I took the prisoner afterwards; they were about five minutes' walk from the prosecutor's.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 14. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-214]
1017. BENJAMIN PHEASEY was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of March, 3 quarters of a pint of rum, value 1s. 3d., the goods of William King, his master.
CHARLES IVEY. I am an officer. I was sent for to Mr. King's public-house; Mr. King's sister charged the prisoner with taking three quarterns of rum; she said, in his presence, that she saw him draw it from the cask - he did not deny it; I took him into custody, and as I took him along he threatened the prosecutor that he would do for him and his sister also; he said he had taken the rum, that they could only send him to Newgate, but could not hang him.
FRANCES KING. I am the prosecutor's sister - the prisoner was his servant. I did not see him draw the rum, but I saw him with three-quarterns of rum - he was going to take it out into the yard with some empty pots, which he had in his hand; he had the rum in another pot - no customer had ordered it.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me draw it? A. No, I heard you draw it: I was alone in the bar, and when I looked I saw you setting the mug down - the tap leaks, and there is a half-pint pot under it to catch the leakings, but you drew this in an ale pint, which is not used for the leakage - he took it up, and was going away with it; I called to him, and asked what he was about - he could not have taken it by mistake.
Prisoner. When I cleared the counter down there was a quantity of pots; this one had liquor in it; I did not know what it was till she examined it, and found it was rum - she asked where I got it; I made her no answer. Witness. I did not hear him draw it, but I heard a particular noise in the tap.
WILLIAM KING. I came home at ten o'clock - my sister informed me she had caught the prisoner taking the rum; that he had gone out to collect his pots, instead of which he had taken his clothes away by throwing them over the next wall, and absconded - I was in search of him till nine o'clock at night, when I found him asleep at a public-house in Jewin-street; I told him the charge; he swore and abused me very much - he had been thirteen months in my employ; I took him in out of charity, and gave him 4s. a week; he had not a farthing when he came to me.
HENRY BERRESFORD. (Policeman G 8.) The prisoner was brought to the station-house; he said, "I took the rum, but they can only send me to Newgate;" he objected to be searched; at last he threw down 4l. in gold, 7s. 6d. in silver, and a watch.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-215]
Before Mr. Recorder.
1018. THOMAS ROBERTS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 1 pair of boots, value 6s., the goods of Samuel Woolston.
SAMUEL WOOLSTON. I am a shoemaker, and live in St. Giles'. On the 20th of February I was going up the shop, and caught a glance of the prisoner going out of the shop; I went out, and found him within twenty yards of the door, with these boots, which hung by the side of the counter.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. Two boys who were running threw the boots down; I took them up.
GUILTY. Aged 12. - Transported for seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-216]
Second London Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
1079. RICHARD HOLTOM was indicted for not surrendering to a certain commission of bankruptcy, which had been awarded and issued against him.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS AND PHILLIPS conducted the prosecution.
THOMAS DUFFIELD produced a commission of bankruptcy issued against the prisoner, on the 28th of December, 1829, also the adjudication, the Gazette, and proceedings.
JOHN JOSEPH PARLOUR. I was apprenticed to the prisoner. When I first went to him he carried on the business of a surgeon and apothecary, and lived in Albany-street, Regent's-park; he afterwards moved to Charlotte-street, and carried on the same business there - he got his drugs from the chemists, made them up, and sent them out to customers; he sold them - Mr. Dodson came to live there; I think the plate with the prisoner's name was not on the door after Mr. Dodson came - the plate was removed; I cannot say when.
COURT. Q. When did the prisoner cease to reside in Charlotte-street? A. As near as I can say, about the beginning of November, 1829.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. Did you remain with him till he left that place? A. I did; I never saw him there after Mr. Dodson came - I remained there; I do not know exactly how long Dodson might have been in the house when the name was removed - I cannot say whether it was there at all after Dodson came; the prisoner had no shop - there was a surgery where we made up medicines for patients, which were entered to them in the day-book, and then posted into the ledger; the bills were made out, and some of them, I think, were paid.
Q. Did persons come to the house to buy drugs? A. A person named Sherman, a surgeon, once ordered medicines at our house, which he had made up for him; they were entered in the day-book to him - he lived in the house with the prisoner the latter part of the time, but not at that time; he was in the habit of coming occasionally - he visited some of the prisoner's patients, and gave directions during his absence.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Was it before or after he came there, that he ordered medicines for his own patients? A. I think it was while Mr. Holtom lived in the house; sherman then lived in Park-street.
COURT. Q. While you were there, did anybody come to buy any thing? A. I never sold any thing, nor do I know of any thing being sold to person who came.
MARGARET BROWN. I am a widow, and lived in Lower John-street, Commercial-road. A person, who went by the name of Captain Mitchell, took apartments at my house the latter end of 1829; the prisoner used to visit him, and when he first came to my house, he (the prisoner) left his name asCaptain Miller - Mitchell left my house at two o'clock one Sunday afternoon, to go to France, and left a paper, signed"Captain Hall;" I never spoke to the prisoner about it - the prisoner always answered to the name of Miller; I am certain of the prisoner's person - he had not mustachios when he visited at my house; he used to wear a blue frock coat - he (the prisoner)had a quantity of boxes at my house; I saw him open one case - it contained horse harness; there was a hamper of medicine - he took the medicine out of it, and put it into a wooden box; I heard him say he had a phaeton, which he wanted to place at the George, half-way-house, Commercial-road - the week before Mitchell left my house, a van of property was brought there, and a backney-coach came on the Saturday-night; the prisoner told me he was going to France, in the steam packet, with Mitchell and his wife, who he called Mrs. Miller - Mitchell left my house in a backney-coach, the very same morning as the prisoner left; Mitchell used to carry pistols in his pocket - I never saw the prisoner with any thing of the kind.
COURT. Q. Can you fix the date of his leaving on Sunday morning? A. I am sure it was the latter end of November, 1829.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. You cannot recollect whether it was on the 22nd? A. I cannot - the prisoner did not bring the van of goods.
COURT. Q. Did the box he opened come in the van? A. Yes; he told me it was medicine - there were bottles and jars.
THOMAS MAGNUS CATLIN. I am a solicitor - Mr. Dodson was a client of mine. In October, 1829, in consequence of an advertisement, I saw the prisoner, and entered into a negociation with him for the purchase of his business as a surgeon and apothecary, in Charlotte-street, Portland-place; the purchase was completed - Mr. Dodson was a young man; the prisoner was to remain in the house for three weeks after the purchase - I believe that part of the arrangement was inserted in the written agreement, which I have not got; I saw him at the house on the Monday after the treaty was completed, which was on Saturday - (I have ceased to be Mr. Dodson's solicitor, and have given up all the papers;) this was on the 16th of November - the prisoner's name remained on the door for three or four weeks after that; it was taken down some time after the docket was struck - I only saw him in the house two days after the treaty was completed; I was present at a conversation between Dodson and the prisoner that day; Mr. Dodson asked him when he would pay him the 118l. which he owed him - he said he had got nothing but bankers' bills, but that he was going into the City the next morning, and would either pay Mr. Dodson, or call at my house and leave it with me - at this time the prisoner wore more the habit of a quaker than any thing else; he was not like a captain - I never saw him after that Monday; I struck a docket against him on the 24th of December - we endeavoured to arrest him first, but could not; immediately we found he had gone abroad we struck the docket - that was our reason for striking it; I am not aware that the debt of 118l. had any reference to the negociation about the business; he had told me on the 3rd of November that the debt he owed Dodson should be paid on the completion of the business - the amount was then mentioned as 118l.; it had then been agreed that Dodson should have the business, for which 700l. in all was to be paid - Dodson's mother was to pay for the business.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. The debt the prisoner acknowledged he owed Dodson was 118l.? A. I believe it was - I understood so from Dodson and from the bankrupt; I am not aware that Dodson and the prisoner were acquainted previous to my answering the advertisement - I think I first saw them together in September - I think it was after I had written to the prisoner respecting this purchase; Dodson was not above twenty-two or twenty-three years old.
Q. How much money was to be given for the business? A. I do not know - several arrangements were made, and alterations; Holtom first asked 400l. for the business and lease, but an alteration was made and a valuation, and the sum actually paid was 700l., including fixtures, wine, and furniture - I cannot tell the particular items; they were all classed together - I believe I wrote Mrs. Dodson one or two letters; I know Mr. Dodson was living respectably, and believe he was able to lend 118l. - I am attesting witness to the agreement; it was not stamped - (Looking at four letters) these are my handwriting; I was present when Mr. Camphell, a friend of Mrs. Dodson's, paid the 700l., on behalf of Messrs. Postlethwaite, her bankers, on Saturday, the 14th of November.
Q. Now, do not you know that the difference between the first agreement and the second was just 118l? A. Not that I am aware of; I never knew it.
Q. Do not you know that the second agreement was a fraudulent one, made for the purpose of deceiving Mrs. Dodson, to get the 118l.; and that it was 118l. more than the real purchase-money? A. I do not; I never knew it from Dodson or any body; there was a different arrangement made, and I was informed by both parties, that the sum to be inserted in the deed was 700l.; and, to the best of my knowledge, that was the bona fide sum for the purchase; Mr. Dodson went up to take possession of the business on Saturday, the 14th: the prisoner was to introduce him to his patients for three weeks at least.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was a verbal arrangement entered into as to the time Holtom was to remain on the premises after the valution? A. Certainly; he agreed to reside on the premises for three weeks from the 14th of November Mr. Dodson was merely to lodge with him during that time.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. When the 700l. was paid to the prisoner, was any demand made on him for the 118l.? A. No; we were at Mr. Campbell's house in Friday-street, Cheapside; Mr. Campbell handed the money to Holtom, in Dodson's presence and in mine - I went to my office; I believe Dodson and the prisoner went to Charlotte-street together - the money was paid in bankers' acceptances, and on Monday the prisoner said he was going into the City next morning, and would leave the amount with me or pay Dodson.
EDWARD HARRISON LINCARD. I am a surgeon. About three weeks or a month before the prisoner's final settlement with Mr. Dodson, he told me he was in difficulties, and was about selling his business, and wished to gain time with his creditors till he had sold it.
Q. Did he ever make any request to you? A. He asked me to allow him to bring some boxes to my house, ashe was going to Van Dieman's Land; I agreed - some boxes and trunks came to my house in Aldermanbury; there might be a dozen boxes and one or two portmanteaus - on a Sunday afterwards he told me the business between him and Dodson had been settled on the Saturday; he wished the boxes to remain at my house - he gave no other reason than that my house was nearer the docks, and he had given up his premises; I know one Jordan was a creditor of his - I suspected the prisoner was about to leave the country, and at my request Jordan came to my house.
Cross-examined. A. Did you go to his house in Charlotte-street, after he told you the business was settled? A. Not to my recollection; the plate, with his name on it, was brought to my house very shortly before the prisoner went away; the last time I saw him was the Monday evening following the Saturday on which he said the business was settled - I believe he brought the plate himself; I know Mr. Dodson, but did not then.
MR. CATLIN re-examined. I was present before the commissioners at the time of the adjudication; (the witness proved that the several commissioners qualified, &c.); I attended the third meeting - the bankrupt did not surrender at any of the meetings; I attended till three o'clock, and heard him proclaimed.
Cross-examined by MR. CALARKSON. Q. Was you Dodson's attorney when he made an affidavit of debt against the bankrupt? A. Certainly; I prepared all the proceedings, and became his attorney when he acted as assignee - he was removed afterwards; I did not appear for him in the Court of Chancery - he has since taken the benefit of the Insolvent Act; I opposed his discharge for his creditors, not on my own behalf - he was in my debt; I was his attorney till about eight months before he took the benefit of the Act - I never knew of there being two valuations made in this negociation; there were several arrangements - the original valuation of Mr. Close's was not altered that I am aware of; there was a different arrangement altogether after the valuation - I never heard that 118l. of the purchase money was to be repaid to Dodson; I believe there was some wine in the house - I am not aware, at this length of time, whether I ever told the prisoner on no account to mention to Dodson about the wine - (looking at a letter) this is in my writing - there was no secret transaction about the wine.
Q. Did you ever write to the prisoner, "Do not say a word to Dodson about the wine; I shall insert it in the agreement, and then inform him upon execution that I agreed to take it for him with the furniture?" A. If I have written that in the letter, I have; I am not aware how it could have arisen; (looking at the letter) if this be my writing I did write so; it is my writing - I am not aware what it relates to, or what may have been my motive, at this lapse of time - Dodson went over the stock; the only reason I can suppose is that Dodson must have objected to take the wine, it being a larger stock than he required; he was my client - I cannot say what quantity of wine there was; there was a very large quantity I know - I can give no reason for writing this, except that he would very likely object to take so large a stock - (letter read).
DEAR SIR, - I hasten to enclose you a letter I have received from my client, Mr. Dodson, by which you will perceive he will be here to settle the business on Monday or Tuesday; as soon as he arrives my clerk shall come and inform you what hour he will wait on you to look over the books, and satisfy himself about the business - we will then return here, and enter into the agreement. - Do not say a word to Dodson about the wine; I shall insert it in the agreement, and then inform him, on execution, that I agreed to take it for him with the furniture. - You know I am perfectly satisfied about the business.
Q. Could you have any honest reason for writing that? A. Holtom made it part of the arrangement that the wine should be taken; I had no opportunity of seeing Dodson, and presume I wrote the letter to state I would agree to take the wine, and insert it in the agreement and get Mr. Dodson's consent to take it - his consent was obtained before the agreement was signed.
COURT Q. Then why did you wish the prisoner not to tell him about it? A. Because I would explain it to him- I did not mean to entrap him; if he objected I would take it all myself, and I did - he objected to take so much- I said I would take the rest at a valuation; no regular quantity was inserted in the agreement.
Q. You say, "You know I am perfectly satisfied about the business, and you score the letter I?" A. I was satisfied myself about the purchase of the business, and it was subject to the approval of Mr. Dodson; this letter is my writing - (read.)
To R. Holtom, Esq.
October 28th., 1820.
DEAR SIR, - I have seen Mr. Postlethwaite, who has been here, and will have the money ready; I have given him a letter of introduction to you - he will call; recollect the purchase-money 400l., valuation of fixtures 27ll. 10s., medicines 28l. 10s. - do not let him see your agreement: he has seen the one signed by you; I enclose you a note I have this instant received from Dodson, by which he fears Postlethwaite will write to Mrs. Dodson before the business is closed, but if you tell him you must have the business closed to-morrow, it will be done; do not let this letter be seen - tell Postlethwaite you must have the agreement completed to-morrow, in consequence of the illness of your wife.
Q. Now, on your oath, was his wife ill at that time? A. I am sure I cannot tell; if I have written that, I believe she was ill, but I cannot positively say.
Q. What do you mean by "Do not let Postlethwaite see your agreement?" A. Because I presumed Dodson had made a different arrangement with him than the first agreement alluded to - a paper was signed by the prisoner.
MR. ADOLPHUS. We have no evidence of the petitioning creditor's debt but this witness; if the Court think his evidence is doubtful, I will withdraw from the prosecution.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-217]
1020. THOMAS STONE was indicted for a misdemeanor.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-218]
First London Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1021. BRIDGET CARNEY was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MARY EDMONDS. I am a servant out of place. On the 14th of February I was walking along Bishopsgate-street, and felt something touch my foot; I turned round, and the prisoner, who I never saw before, picked up ared purse; she told me to hold my tongue, and say nothing, but to go with her - she took me under a gateway, produced the purse from her bosom, pulled a ring from it, and asked me what she was to do with it; I said the best way to tell the value was to go where they sold such things; she said No, if I would give her a trifle, I might take it - I asked what I should give her; she asked what I had got - I asked if 5s. would do; she said No, not for a gold ring; I asked if 6s. would do - she said, was that all; I said it was all I bad in my pocket except a few coppers - she asked me to give her them also- I gave her 6s. 6d., and took the ring; she said it was gold; I took it home, and found it was of no value; on the 2nd of March I was accidentally in Bishopsgate-street again; she came up, and tapped me on the shoulder, told me to hold my tongue, and not say any thing; she showed me a purse - I said, "You good for nothing wretch, you are the woman that took me in before;" she insulted me with most abusive language - I went away, and in the evening I was going along she crossed the road, stood before me, and said, "Look here, what a fine purse I have found, what's in it?" I took no notice of her, but she was taken into custody.
WILLIAM BOSTON. I am an officer. The prisoner was brought to the watch-house by a gentleman; I have the ring; I took it to a silversmith, who says it is not worth the sixteenth part of a farthing.
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw her till she gave me in charge, and said I had asked her to buy a ring, which is false.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-219]
Third Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin
1022. WILLIAM ROGERS was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 2 shirts, value 6s., the goods of George Goodwin.
GEORGE GOODWIN. I am conductor of an omnibus. I went to an eating-house in Bell-street, Marylebone, and came out, leaving two shirts in a bundle behind me; I returned for it, and it was gone - I did not see the prisoner there.
CHARLES CLARK. I am a Policeman. I met the prisoner about four o'clock in the morning of the 1st of April, in Bury-place, carrying three shirts - I said, "You are the person I have been looking for, for taking these shirts out of a cook-shop, about half-past twelve tonight" - he said, "Here they are;" I secured him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I did not intend to steal them, but to take them home to the prosecutor.
GUILTY. Aged 28. - Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18320405-220]
1023. THOMAS REECE and JOHN BAILEY were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of March, 1 cruet top, value 5s., and 1 spoon, value 5s., the goods of the Rev. Hugh Hodgson; and that the said Thomas Reece had been before convicted of felony.
RICHARD BRITTING. I live at the Rev. Hugh Hodgson, in Dean-street, Westminster. On the 6th of March, between two and three o'clock, I was in the kitchen - something drew my attention to the window, and on going towards the passage, I saw the door open, which I had shut not ten minutes before; I went by the pantry, and missed the top of a mustard-pot and a spoon - I ran up the area, and saw the prisoner walking on the terrace, within twenty yards of the area - I walked past them; I collared Bailey - Reece immediately ran away; I called Stop thief! and he was stopped by Lloyd - he threw the spoon over into the garden, and the mustard top was found in his pocket - nothing was found on Bailey; they were within a yard of each other, and appeared in company.
Cross-examined by MR. HEATON. Q. Had you seen a person loitering about for two or three days before? A. No, I never said so.
EDWARD LLOYD. I heard a cry of Stop thief! saw Reece running, and took him in custody - he threw the spoon out of his hand, and the mustard-top was found in his pocket.
DAVID PHILLIPS. I received the prisoners in charge, and found the mustard top in Reece's waistcoat pocket.(Property produced and sworn to.)
EDMUND CHICHELEY, I am a Policeman. I have a certificate of the former conviction of Reece; I was a witness on his trial, and know him to be the man - (read).
REECE - GUILTY. Aged 24.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
BAILEY - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-221]
1024. MARY RODENWINCH was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 4 sovereigns, 1 half-sovereign, and five 5l. Bank notes, the property of William Fenwick, from his person; against the Statute, &c.
WILLIAM FENWICK. I belong to the Juno. On the 13th of March I found the prisoner at the sign of the Crown, where I went to have some refreshment - she asked me to treat her, which I did; I went to the Match-walk with her, to a house - I agreed to stay all night with her; I had changed one 5l. note, and put the change into my purse - I had five other 5l. notes there; I am sure it was all safe when I got to the room - I put my purse in my trousers, and put them on a chair; we went to bed at near twelve o'clock - I awoke about seven, and she was gone; I looked into my trousers, and the money and purse were gone - I gave information; I am sure she is the girl.
Prisoner. Q. You know you went with two girls, and bade me good night? A. No - I am sure you are the girl.
HENRY LENNON (Police-constable, K 98.) The captain told me he had been robbed, and gave a description of the girl - I went and searched the house, and found this purse between the sheet and bolster, in the bed down stairs; the prosecutor said it was the bed the girl took him to.
MARY DAVIS. I am bar-maid at the Crown. I saw the prisoner and the prosecutor there; I changed a 5l. note for him, and he put the money safe before he left the bar; the prisoner was not at the bar a when he took his change, but they went out together - they were both intoxicated.
GEORGE PHILLIPS. About half-past twelve o'clock on the 13th of March I met the prisoner and the captain in the highway; he said to me, "This girl is following me, and I can't get rid of her;" I turned her away, and he said he was going to Lower Shadwell - in ten minutes I saw them together again, going towards the Match-walk.(Purse produced and sworn to)
Prisoner's Defence. He asked me to have part of a pint of half-and-half; he then asked if I would have another - I said Yes; he then gave me a glass of gin - when we came out he asked where I lived; I said not about there - the Policeman said, "Go on," and I went one way and he another - two girls then came up, one an Irish and one and English girl; he went away with them - I never saw him again.
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Fourteen Years.

1025. ABRAHAM BOLTON was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of March, 1 shawl, value 10s., the goods of William Mote and another; to which he pleaded

1026. FRANCES ANN NASH and WILLIAM PEARSON were indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 1 lace collar, value 7s., the goods of Ruth Bruce.
ELIZABETH BRUCE. I live with my mother, Ruth Bruce. About two years ago Nash came to our house, and passed as Pearson's husband; she had lived with my aunt some years ago. On the 20th of February they came, and my mother asked them to stop to tea; my mother showed them a lace tippet which laid on the sofa - they said it was very handsome; my mother then put it into a drawer, and went out to get some tea; Pearson then asked me to show Nash the wash-house and back garden, but she did not go - I went down, leaving them in the room; when my mother came back they had tea, and went away about six o'clock; we missed the collar in a few minutes - I made inquiries, and found where Nash lived; I went and charged her with it - she denied it, and I called the officer.
JOHN BENNETT. I am an officer. I took Nash about eleven o'clock on the 27th of February, and in the morning I went to her lodging; from information I received I went to the water-closet, and with a long search I found on the soil three pieces of a duplicate, which makes the duplicate complete, all but the pawnbroker's name.
JAMES ALDOUS. I am a pawnbroker. I have a lace tippet, pawned by Nash, in the name of Ann Lewis - I have known her a long time.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Nash. This man knows nothing of it; I took it because I was out of work, and literally starving; my landlady said if I could not pay my rent that night I should be turned into the street.
NASH - GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.
PEARSON - GUILTY. Aged 30.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-224]
1027. JOHN OWEN was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of March, 8 window-sashes, value 5l., and 2 doors, value 10s., the goods of Thomas Wade, and fixed to a building of his; against the Statute, &c.
JOHN BARNEY DAY. I am agent to Mr. Thomas Wade- he has three houses in Fox-court, St. Sepulchre. On the 20th of March I met the prisoner carrying these eight sashes and two doors, which had been fixed in Mr. Wade's houses; I asked what he was going to do with them - he said to take them to Barbican, and two persons had employed him to carry them there, but he did not know who - I said I should detain the sashes; I found on him a chisel, which the officer compared with the marks on the frame, and it fitted them exactly; these are the articles.
STEPHEN LLOYD. I am an officer. I took the prisoner- I fitted this chisel to the marks on the frame and door posts.
Prisoner's Defence. I was employed to carry them.
GUILTY. Aged 39. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-225]
1028. WILLIAM PRESTON and WILLIAM PHILLIPS were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of March, 5s. in copper monies numbered, the monies of Caroline Burgess.
CAROLINE BURGESS. I am single - I keep a general shop, in George-street, Foley-place. On the 1st of March, about seven o'clock in the morning, Preston came to my shop alone, and asked for some sugar-candy - he then changed his mind to sugar-plums; he asked the price of bread; I told him, and said he should have half a loaf if he pleased - he said he would go up the street, and get the money; he went out - I shut the door; he then came back, and said he could not have the bread - he made a leap on the counter; he took up the sugar-plums, for which he put down a halfpenny; I was aware he had taken something, but I did not know what - I went after him, and saw him join Phillips about eight doors off; I returned to my shop, and looked to see if I had lost any weights, but I had not; the officer came afterwards, and asked if I had lost any thing; I then looked, and missed a 5s. paper of halfpence, which had been done up the day before, to pay the baker.
THOMAS BUTTRESS. I am an officer. I took, Phillips in Cleveland-street, nearly a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's, at a quarter before eight o'clock in the morning; I took Preston the next day - I found 2s. 5 1/2d. on Phillips; Preston had spent his - Phillips said a boy had stolen 5s. worth of halfpence from a chandler's shop, in George-street, and he had half of it; he led me to the shop.
PRESTON - GUILTY. Aged 10.
PHILLIPS - GUILTY. Aged 12.
Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18320405-226]
1029. NICHOLAS PHILIP was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of February, 1 window-sash, containing 8 panes of glass, value 8s., the goods of James Staley, and fixed to a building of his; against the Statute, &c.
JOHN CALLER. I am an officer. On the 23rd of February, I met the prisoner in St. Pancras old road, about two hundred yards from the prosecutor's, carrying this sash; I asked where he was going with it - he said, "To Pitfield-street, Hoxton;" that he had brought it from Mr. Smith's in Clare-market - I took him, and went to inquire for Smith, but could find no such person; I then went to the buildings, and found where it had been taken from - it fits exactly.
JAMES IRVINE. This belongs to an empty house of Mr. James Taylor's I had seen the prisoner on the works, making sashes for Mr. Taylor, but he had been out of employ for about a fortnight.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in Pancras-road, and sawthe sash there; I was going to take it to a public-house, to find the owner - I thought I should get a few half-pence.
GUILTY. Aged 52.
Confined One Month in Newgate.
[trialAccount t18320405-227]
1030. ANN PAVEY was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of January, 1 blanket, value 2s.; 2 towels, value 6d.; 2 pillows, value 5s.; 1 spoon, value 2s.; 2 sheets, value 4s.; 2 candlesticks, value 2s.; 1 table-cloths, value 1s. 6d., and 1 counterpane, value 3s., the goods of Jane Bull.
JANE BULL. I am a widow, and live in Tooke's-court. I let part of my house to a person named Tetsoll; the prisoner was an aunt of theirs, and visited them - they ran away in my debt; on the 28th of January I went into their room, and missed the property stated - I made inquiry, and on Monday night they sent me the duplicates, in a letter, by post.
WILLIAM HUMPHRIES. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Skinner-street. I have two towels, a blanket, a counterpane, two pillows, two candlesticks, and a table-cloth, which were pawned by the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. DONNE. Q. When were they pawned? A. Between the 17th of December, and the 24th of January; I have received part of them before, in the name of Tetsoll - I believe the prisoner is their aunt.
THOMAS HUETSON. I have a silver spoon, pawned by the prisoner, on the 18th of January.
GEORGE BARBER. I am an officer. I received the prisoner, and got the duplicates from the prosecutrix.
MRS. BULL. These are my property; the prisoner had been upwards of three months with her nephew and niece.
Cross-examined. Q. Were these things never pawned to pay your rent? A. Not to my knowledge; I had seen the prisoner's husband come there, but not after these goods were gone - I said I had no wish to prosecute, if I could get my things, and I was offered the money - this is the letter I received (read.)
MADAM, - It is with feelings of very great pain I now address you, but I can assure you it was distress and trouble made me leave your house, and induced me to act in the way I have done; but you may rely on it, as I hope to be saved, in a very short time, I will pay you, and make all things right. I cannot say more, but you shall hear from me again very soon.
Your's, obediently, J. TETSOLL.
Prisoner's Defence. My niece was very ill, and I went there now and then; they were in great distress, and were in the habit of pawning the things the whole year round - they pawned these things to pay the rent, or Mrs. Bull said she would play the deuce with them; they expected 10l. of a gentleman, who owed them 30l. - I did not know of their going away.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-228]
1031. ELIZABETH POWELL was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of February, 3 table-cloths, value 9s.; 2 petticoats, value 3s.; 3 shirts, value 9s.; 3 shifts, value 9s.; 4 handkerchiefs, value 7s., and 1 apron, value 6d., the goods of Matthew Fletcher.
SARAH FLETCHER. I am the wife of Matthew Fletcher- he lives in John's-place, Islington-green; I take in washing - the prisoner was in my service; I gave her 7s. a week, and her board, but she did not sleep there. I missed some articles on the 25th of February. and asked her about them - she denied it; I sent her home with a basket of linen, and that she did not carry home.
GEORGE ELLIS. I am a Police-constable. On the 27th of February the prisoner was given into my custody- I searched her, and saw her throw away the duplicates of this property.
THOMAS HARVEY. I am a pawnbroker. I have a number of articles pawned by the prisoner.
GEORGE BARBER. I am a pawnbroker. I have two shifts and three handkerchiefs pawned by the prisoner -I gave her these duplicates.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that she had pledged the articles by the prosecutrix's desire.
SARAH FLETCHER. I never sent her to pawn any thing.
GUILTY. Aged 47. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-229]
1032. JOSEPH BLUMSON and MARY THOMPSON were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of March, 1 shawl, value 4l., the goods of Joseph Horner.
MR. DOWLING conducted the prosecution.
SARAH ELIZABETH ROBERTSON. I am shop-woman to Mr. Joseph Horner, of Myrtle-street, Hoxton - he is a shawl-dyer. On the 14th of March I missed a shawl off the counter, between five and six o'clock in the evening; this is it - here is a number on it, which I put myself.
Cross-examined by MR. DONNE. Q. When did you see it last? A. About ten minutes before - I was not in the shop all the time; we do not sell shawls.
WILLIAM EDWARDS. I am shopman to Mr. Sowerby, a pawnbroker, in Brick-lane. At a quarter-past seven o'clock on the evening of Wednesday, the 14th of March, Thompson came to our shop, offered this shawl, and wanted 10s. on it; I asked whose it was - she said Mrs. Smith's, No. 3, over the way; I said there was no such number there - she then said down King-street, No. 3, or No. 9; I put the shawl under the counter, sent: for the Policeman, and gave her in charge - this is it; Blumson came to our house, and said, "Have you not sent a woman to the station-house?" I said Yes; he said, "I bought the shawl about two years ago;" I said, "The pattern has not been up so long;" he then said it was eighteen months ago, and he bought it of a man at his door - he then asked me the value of it; I said about 2l. 10s. - he said,"That is what I gave for it."
WILLIAM KING CLARK. I am a Police-constable, and took Thompson. I asked her who the shawl belonged to - she said Mrs. Smith, and she would take me to her; she took me to No. 3, Matthew's-buildings, where I saw Blumson laying on a bed - she said to him, "Joe, get up and fetch Mrs. Smith;" he got up, and went away, but did not return - I took her to the station, and soon after the pawnbroker and Blumson came; Blumson there stated that he had bought the shawl eighteen months ago for 30s. - I have seen the prisoners together very often.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say who he bought it of? A. Yes, of a hawking pedlar, who came to his door- Blumson was out on bail.
JOSEPH HORNER. The shawl is my property; I missed it on the Wednesday,
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see either of the prisoner's near your house? A. No.
JOHN FOSTER DAWSON. I am book-keeper to Mr. Horner. I know the shawl was on the counter, half a yard from the door.
JAMES BROWN. I am an officer of Worship-street. I was at the office when the prisoners were brought there; Blumson said he bought the shawl between eighteen months and two years ago, and at the second examination, when he had heard the value of it, he said he gave 5l. for it.
BLUMSON - GUILTY. Aged 21.
THOMPSON - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-230]
1033. JANE BRADLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of January, 4 caps, value 10s., and 3 yards of lace, value 3s., the goods of Ann Druett; 1 cap, value 2s.; 1 apron, value 1d.; 1 pair of clogs, value 6d.; 1 comb, value 3d.; 1 tea-caddy, value 1d.; 1 butter-pot, value 1d.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 3d.; 1 pair of stockings, value 1d.; 1 bell-rope, value 1d.; 1 petticoat, value 6d., and 1 necklace, value 6d., the goods of Elizabeth Suckling .
ANN DRUETT. I am the daughter of Elizabeth Suckling - she lives at Upper Clapton. The prisoner lodged at her house gratis, because she did a few things for her; I understood from my mother that the prisoner left her without notice - I did not live with my mother, but had left the articles stated as mine at my mother's house.
ROBERT MESSENGER. I am an officer. I apprehended the prisoner by direction of Mr. Suckling - I told her I came respecting some things, which I had a list of: she said she had got them, and I found them there - they had not been parted with.
WILLIAM FYNNEY BULLOCK. I am an officer. I went with Messenger, and took this property.
ANN DRUETT. These four caps and this lace are mine- I can identify some of the other articles as my mother's, and I believe they all are hers.
Prisoner. These caps I had given me by her mother to make. Witness. These thick caps I gave my mother to get made, and the others were there to bleach; the prisoner was employed to make some of them, but she worked at my mother's, and slept there till she went away - she used to light the fire, and do little jobs, as my mother is an invalid, and she used to take in work for other people.
GUILTY. Aged 19.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury. - Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18320405-231]
1034. CHARLES SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 32 mugs, value 2s. 6d., the goods of George Matthews.
JOHN BATTSON. I live with Mr. George Matthews - he keeps a china-shop in White Lion-street, Pentonville. These mugs were taken from our door; the prisoner was followed to several streets, and taken with them.
GEORGE KING. I saw the prisoner unbook these things from the prosecutor's door, and walk away very steadily with them hanging before him, to the end of the street; he then hung them on his shoulder, and walked on nearly a quarter of a mile - I gave him into custody, and he gave them up.
JOHN SHEEHY. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and have the property.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was in the greatest distress -I had called two hours before on the overseers, and they refused me any relief, because I had slept out of the parish the night before.
GUILTY. Aged 24. - Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18320405-232]
1035. WILLIAM TATE was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of March, 4 pictures, value 20s.; 2 pairs of pistols, value 2l., and 2 fenders, value 4s., the goods of Edward Wood, his master.
EDWARD WOOD. I live at Kentish-town - the prisoner was my gardener; I discharged him by a month's warning, and he left me on the 28th of February. I had a cottage in my garden, in which were two pairs of pistols, two fenders, and four pictures - I missed them on the 3rd of March, and sent for an officer; I know the pistols had been in my cottage within a fortnight - I have seen these two pictures, which I know were in my cottage.
WILLIAM CLEMENTS. (Police-constable S 98.) I went in search of the prisoner; I found him in Providence-place, Kentish-town: I found a duplicate on him, and this picture I found at Mrs. Brown's, in Cottage-place, Brompton-square.
SARAH BROWN. I am the wife of John Brown - he is a butcher. The prisoner came to my house on the 29th of February; I have known him upwards of twenty years; he brought this picture, and asked me to take care of it for him; he said he had bought it - I gave it to the officer.
MR. WOOD. These are my property.
Cross-examined by MR. DONNE. Q. You were not residing in the cottage? A. No, nor any one else - I had three servants who might have access to it, but the prisoner had more access to it than any one else - the pistols have not been found.
GUILTY. Aged 23. - Transported for Seven Years.
There was another indictment against the prisoner.

1037. MARGARET WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of March, 1 basket, value 1d.; 2 shirts, value 5s.; 2lbs. weight of cheese, value 1s. 6d.; 16 cakes of bread, value 6d., and 2 pieces of linen, value 1s. 6d., the goods of James Chapman.
JAMES CHAPMAN. I took a basket, containing this property, to the Green-gate, a little before nine o'clock on the evening of the 14th of March; I put it on the seat in the tap-room - I had something to drink, and then missed the basket - I did not see the prisoner there when I went in, but I did after it was missing; I sent for a Police-constable, who took the prisoner the same night.
THOMAS COOK. I was there. I saw the prisoner come in and have half a pint of porter; the basket was missed, and five or six of us ran out - I took her with it on her arm.
BENJAMIN GOODWIN. I am an officer. I took the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I go about carrying a basket - I went in with my basket; that was gone, and I took this by mistake.
GUILTY. Aged 45. - Confined One Month.

1038. HENRY THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on 11th of March, 4 pewter pots, value 4s., the goods of John Cox.
THOMAS GOOK. I am an officer. On Sunday morning, the 11th of March, I saw the prisoner and three others near one another - I knew them, and told Wood, who was with me, that something was wrong; I saw four pots standing at Mr. Ponsonby's rails - I watched some time, and saw the other three go away; I followed them some distance, and then ran round to find the prisoner - Wood gave me information; I took the prisoner, and found two pots in his hat and two on his person.
JOHN COX. I keep a public-house, in Pall-mall. These pots are mine.
GUILTY. Aged 22. - Transported for Seven Years.

1039. WILLIAM WYATT was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of March, 1 flageolet, value 4s, the goods of William Newton.
FRANCIS FEATHERSTONE. I am an officer. I was on duty on the morning of the 29th of March, in Plummer's-row, City-road, and saw the prisoner in company with another; they went to several shops, and then to the prosecutor's; they stood ten minutes in front of it, then the prisoner put his hand into the shop window, and took something out, which he put under his apron, and walked away - I suspected he had stolen something, and went after him; when he saw me he ran down Park-street - I called a man to stop him, and when I came up I found this flageolet under his apron - he said he found it under a stall.
GEORGE NEWTON. This is my property, but it was at my father's house; his name is William.
GUILTY. Aged 15. - Confined Six Weeks,
[trialAccount t18320405-237]
1040. ELIZABETH WEBB was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of February, 1 ring, value 10s., the goods of John Smith.
JOHN SMITH. I am a jeweller, and live in the Strand. On the 27th of February the prisoner came to my shop; my man was showing her some rings in a tray, which contained thirty or forty rings - I was looking from a back window, and saw her take a ring from the tray, and after attempting to look at it, put it into her month; the shopman then suspected she had done something with it, but his suspicions were removed by her returning him a brass one; I then sent for an officer, and with some difficulty this ring was got from her mouth.
GEORGE NUTTAN AGERS. I am an officer. I was sent for, and this brass ring was produced at the shop; the shopman said she had stolen a gold one; I asked what she had one with it; she said she had not one - I asked her to open her mouth, which she would not do; I opened it by force and this ring was in it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. When I left home I had a gold ring; I went to buy another - I had money enough to buy it, but I met a man, who took me to have something to drink - it took such effect on me, that I did not know what I did, nor where I was - I had no metal ring when I left home.
GEORGE NUTTAN AGERS. She was no more in liquor than she is now.
GUILTY. Aged 26. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-238]
1041. GEORGE WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of February, 5 jackets, value 40s., the goods of John Driscol.
JOHN DRISCOL. I am a tailor, and live in Four Awl-court, Rosemary-lane. On the 29th of February I had some jackets laying in my place; I missed them about ten o'clock in the morning - I know they had been safe shortly before; I cannot say whether the door was open or not - I went to look for them, and found the prisoner with the jackets, in custody of the officer.
PEARCE DRISCOL. I am an officer. I saw the prisoner in Rosemary-lane - the prosecutor's wife had hold of him, and was crying out for an officer.
CATHERINE DRISCOL. I am the prosecutor's wife. I found the prisoner with this bundle in Rosemary-lane; I asked what he had in it, for he had changed the wrapper; he had come to me two or three days before for a lodging.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was sitting at their house, and smoking my pipe; a shipmate came, and called me out; he had a bundle - he told me to hold it while he went to have a glass of grog; this woman then came up, and asked what I wanted with her bundle; I said, "There is the man who gave it me," but she would not go into the the house to see him.
CATHERINE DRISCOL. He was walking along with the bundle when I came up; he said, "It is a fine morning;" I took hold of his bundle, and said, "I should like to see what is in your bundle, as there was a bundle taken from my house a quarter of an hour ago;" he did not point out any man, but said he did not take them.
GUILTY. Aged 55. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-239]
1042. WILLIAM SMEATON was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of March, 1 pocket-book, value 1d., 1 coat, value, 20s., 1 pillow, value 4s., 1 handkerchief, value 3s., 1 petticoat, value 2s., and 20 duplicates, value 20s., the goods of Robert Oram Woolridge, from the person of Mary, his wife .
MARY WOOLRIDGE. I am the wife of Robert Oram Woolridge, an ironmonger, who lives in Whitecross-street. On the 21st of March I was near Chiswell-street, Finsbury, between one and two o'clock - I was standing on the pavement, and thought of going across the road, but there was a great crowd; I had a pocket-book in my pocket, containing the duplicates of the property stated; a person told me I had had my pocket picked - I went to the station, and found the prisoner.
ROBERT ROBERTSON (Police-constable A 63.) I was on duty, and saw the prosecutrix and some persons abouther; I saw the prisoner lift her gown up, and put his two fore fingers into her pocket, and take out this pocket-book - I grasped at him, but he got from me; I ran after him, and took him with the book in his left-hand pocket - I met with great resistance, and could not have taken him if my brother officer had not come up; about three hundred persons attacked us, and I was almost knocked stupid before I got to the station-house - the prisoner bit a piece out of my hand.
WILLIAM SHEPHERD. I am an officer. I assisted in taking the prisoner - I saw him run across the road, and the officer after him; I saw him passing the book from one pocket to the other - I seized him by one arm, and he threw it down; we were opposed by a great number of persons, and were attacked with sticks, stones, and mud.
Prisoner's Defence. I heard there were two waggons of victuals to be given away, but there was no such thing; I was going home, and saw this old pocket-book - I took it up, and some gentlemen cried out Stop-thief! they came and took me; two or three people said I had picked it up- I said I would go any where; I went to the station, and they asked what I had been doing, and they went round among the people and found this lady.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-240]
1043. GEORGE ABBOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of February, 28 yards of printed cotton, value 14s., the goods of John Jackson and another.
TIMOTHY PRIOR. I live next door to the prosecutor; he is a linen-draper. On the 29th of February I was taking in some articles, and saw the prisoner go and take the cotton from the prosecutor's shop; he was walking away with it, and I took him.
EDWARD BELL. I am an officer; the prisoner and this cotton were given to me.
JOHN JACKSON. This is my property, and was in our shop; I have one partner.
GUILTY. Aged 16 - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-241]
1044. GEORGE ABBOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of March, 4 live tame rabbits, price 4s., the property of Thomas Till.
THOMAS SAMPSON. I live in Hackney-road. On the 2nd of March the prisoner brought two rabbits, and wanted me to purchase them; by the price at which he offered them, I thought he had stolen them; I asked him where he lived, he said against the New church, Hoxton: I said before I paid for them, it was my rule to go and see where people lived - he said what business had I with it; I said, "If you don't let me, I shall give you in charge," which I did, and the rabbits were detained.
TILDEN HARRIS. I am an officer. I was going by; the prisoner was given in charge to me, with these two rabbits.
MORRIS CAREY. I am an officer, I knew the prisoner before, and knew where he lived; I went to his house, and found these other two rabbits.
JANE TILL. I am the wife of Richard Till - we live in Globe-lane. These four rabbits are ours - they had been kept in a stable which was locked; it was broken open on the 2nd of March, and the rabbits stolen.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-242]
1045. FRANCES CRAWLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 3 sovereigns, the monies of William Alison, from his person, and that she had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM ALISON. I am a tailor. I was at the bottom of Tottenham-court-road, near the brewery, with Mr. Maclaren - it was after twelve o'clock at night, on the 17th of March; I had called on my friend, and he was going home with me; we met the prisoner with a plate in her hand, and she said if we would go home with her, she would find us a house to get some drink - we went with her to a house in St. Giles'; I do not know the street; there was another girl in the room, laying on the bed - the prisoner then asked me for money to get something to drink; I took out my money, and gave her a sixpence from among three sovereigns, which was all I had; I returned the three sovereigns to my pocket - my friend then went out with the other girl to get some porter, and the prisoner put her hand into my pocket; I heard my money rattle - I told her to hold off; she then said they were coming, and went to the door, as I thought, to let them in, but instead of that, she ran away - I went after her, but could not see which way she took; I gave notice to the officer, and she was taken the next night - no one else could have taken it.
PETER MACLAREN. The prosecutor called on me that afternoon, and as we were going home, the prisoner came up to us, and took us to Church-lane; we found one girl on the bed - that girl and I went out for some porter; we returned in three or four minutes, and the prisoner was gone - I went to the watch-house, and gave the alarm, and when I came back, the other woman had gone off, and locked the door with a padlock.
EDMUND DAVIS. I am an officer. On the morning of the 17th of March the prosecutor said he had been robbed, and described the prisoner; I knew her - I found her the same night - she had a new cap, and some new lace on, and a had shilling and eleven good ones -I took her about half-past nine o'clock on the Saturday evening, and the robbery had happened about one in the morning.
MAURICE NICHOLAS. I am an officer. I have a certificate of the former conviction of the prisoner, which I got from Mr. Clark's office; I was the officer who took her, and know she is the person - (read).
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw the prosecutor till I was at the station-house; I had been out washing that day, and the money I had I had worked hard for - I was here before, but was as innocent as I am now.
GUILTY. Aged 22. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-243]
1046. GRACE DRAPER was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of March, 1 watch, value 6l., the goods of Thomas Sully, from his person.
THOMAS SULLY. I am a servant out of place. On the 16th of March I met the prisoner a little before twelve o'clock at night; I had been drinking, but knew what I was doing - she asked me to give her something to drink; I said I had no money; she said."I dare say you have got money," and kept following me; I put my hand into my pocket, and found a few coppers and a sixpence; I took out the sixpence, went into a gin-shop, threw it downon the counter, and gave her something to drink - she then came out, but still kept close to me; I desired her to keep off; she then turned into an alley, and said, "This is as near a way as any for you to go" (I had told her I was going to Old-street); I went up there, and she turned into a little court, and made a snatch at my watch, took it out, and ran into the street - I met an officer, and told him of it - he said he knew her perfectly well.
JURY. Q. How came you to be led up the alley? A. I am a stranger in town; she ran away in a moment; she struck me with her hand at the time; - I did not pursue her; I was quite a stranger in the place.
EDWARD BARRINGTON. I am an officer. I know the prisoner very well, by seeing her about Shereditch and Norton-falgate; the prosecutor described her to me; she did not appear on her regular beat after that - she was taken ten days after.
WILLIAM LOWE. I am an officer. I know the prisoner by seeing her about there for three months - she did not appear on her usual beat after this.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-244]
1047. GEORGE EWEN and RICHARD BARTLETT were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 1 pair of boots, value 1s. 6d., and 1 hat, value 7s., the goods of James Mele, from the person of Frederick Mele .
JAMES MELE. I live in Farrier's-rents, Bishopsgate; my son Frederick is four years and a half old. On the 12th of March I came home at four o'clock, and my child was missing; I had seen him at twelve o'clock, when I had been at home - I found him at five o'clock, but he had no hat or shoes.
JANE MEYER. I live with my uncle at the Dyer's Arms, in Long-alley. On the 12th of March I was at my uncle's window, and saw the two prisoners pass, carrying this child - Bartlett had it; it had a hat and a pair of boots on at that time - I thought all was not right, and told Mr. Roberts, who went in pursuit of them - I saw Ewen taken into custody.
ISAAC ROBERTS. I am a baker; I live five doors from Miss Meyer. I went into the house to smoke my pipe; while I was there Miss Meyer told me of this, and said it was not all right - she then went out, came in again, and said,"They are stripping the child;" I went out, and Ewen had come away - I saw Bartlett and the child on the ground: I said to Ewen, "What have you been doing to that child?" he said nothing; I pulled his coat aside, and took these boots from him - he said a boy gave them to him; I got a man to look for Bartlett, but he was gone; a young man took the child, and I took the boots to the officer, who found the parents.
JAMES BROWN. I am an officer. On the day stated, the witness brought Ewen and the boots to me; I took Bartlett on the Saturday following - I cannot find the hat.(Shoes produced and sworn to.)
Bartlett's Defence. I was at my master's at the time of the robbery.
EWEN - GUILTY. Aged 15.
BARTLETT - GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
There was another indictment against the prisoners.

1048. DANIEL HILL was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of March, 4 brass weights, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Jacob Edward Joseph.
JACOB EDWARD JOSEPH. I keep a broker's shop in Bell-row. On the 29th of March I was returning home, and saw the prisoner and another at my shop; the prisoner took up the weights from my shop, and before I could get across the road, they had both turned the corner - I ran, and saw the prisoner putting something over some pales; I looked, and saw they were my weights - I took the prisoner.
MICHAEL THOMPSON. I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and have the weights.
GUILTY. Aged 18.

1049. WINIFRED JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of March, 1 gown, value 2s. 6d., the goods of John Davies.
WILLIAM HICKS. I lodge at John Davies', in Whitecross-street. On the 11th of March I was coming down stairs, and saw the prisoner going out from the yard to go into the passage; Mr. Davis asked her where she had been she said for a private purpose; she had the gown on her arm, and he asked what she was going to do with it - she said she took it off the nail, and was going to throw it on the stairs for safety.
JOHN DAVIES. I know the prisoner by sight, but she had no concern with us, and had no business in my yard; she was in liquor.
Prisoner's Defence. I met my husband, whom I had not seen for five years - he is living with another woman; I spoke to him - he did not attend to what I said, but left me in tears; I then met a man and his wife, who asked me to go and take a little refreshment - I had some beer and gin, which totally deprived me of my senses.
GUILTY. Aged 34. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-247]
1050. WILLIAM LIVERSAGE was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 3 shirts, value 15s.; 3 shirt collars, value 1s. 6d.; 10 handkerchiefs, value 10s.; 1 stock, value 2s.; 1 pair of trousers, value 20s., and 1 pair of gloves, value 6d., the goods of George Pilkington.
SARAH BARTON. I was servant to Mr. Collard; he is a clerk in the Bank, and lives at No. 32, Great Castle-street. I had seen the prisoner on the Sunday week before the 26th of March, when he called at our house, to look at the apartments; on the 26th of March he called again about seven o'clock in the evening; my master and mistress were not at home; he told me he had partly engaged the apartments, that he wished to come in on the Wednesday, and he would send in his luggage on the Tuesday, and it would come in the name of the Rev. Mr. Johnson; he then went away, and called again about nine o'clock - he said the coach was gone, and would it make any difference if he took the lodgings from that night; I said No, and asked him to walk in - he came into the parlour, and asked me to light the bed-room fire; I said there was no fire-place there, but I lighted the fire in the parlour - I went up stairs to get his bed-room ready, and when I came down, he asked to wash his hands; I offered him to wash in the back parlour - he said No, he would rather go up stairs; I asked him to walk up, and while he was washing his hands, he told me to go and get him some chops for his supper; I came down, and as I came past, Ishut my mistress' bed-room door - when I came down into the passage, I heard that door open, and the drawers open- I stood in the passage, expecting he would come down with a bundle, but he came down without any thing, and went into the parlour; I then took the child up to bed - I saw three handkerchiefs on the stairs; I came down again, and got the watchman to get the Policeman - the articles stated, had all been taken out of the drawers, and put under the counterpane of the bed, where he was to sleep; they are all Mr. Pilkington's.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. You had seen him once before? A. Yes, about three or four o'clock on the Sunday week; there was no one in the house but the prisoner, I, and the children, who were down stairs - the drawers were in my mistress' room - he told me he was to have the parlour for his use for a short time, and then to have the first floor and back attic - he did not go out after he came, about nine o'clock.
JOHN JOHNSON. I am a Police-constable. I went to the house, and found the prisoner setting by the fire in the front-parlour; he said he was a very respectable man, and offered to give me his card; the witness said she had heard the drawers opened, and part of the property was on the stairs - I saw the three handkerchiefs laying on separate stairs; I went into the back attic bed-room and under the bed I found three shirts, three collars, seven handkerchiefs, a pair of gloves, and the counterpane thrown over them; I took them, then came down, and searched the prisoner; I found on him 15s. 6d.; I then found this black cravat in his pocket, which he said he had put in in a hurry - I took him to the station, and then found he had got on this pair of trousers, which had been taken from the house, over his own.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe he gave you his right address? A. Yes, he did, and from inquiries I have made I have heard the best of character of him.
WILLIAM COLLARD. The prisoner had come to my house on the 18th of March, but he had not spoken to me about the lodging; when I got home on the night in question I missed a pair of trousers belonging to Mr. Pilkington - I went to the station, and they were taken off the prisoner.
GEORGE PILKINGTON. I lodge in the house. These articles are all mine - the linen was in the drawers, and the trousers were in the room.
Cross-examined. Q. When had you left? A. In the morning, to go to chambers - I had seen the trousers the day before.
The prisoner handed in a statement, representing that he had fallen into had company, and had been seduced to commit the offence by another person.
GUILTY. Aged 26. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-248]
1051. WILLIAM JARVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, 2 coats, value 17s., and 1 waistcoat, value 3s., the goods of Haggady Baker.
HAGGADY BAKER. I live at No. 5, Union-row, Great Turnstile. This is my property.
RICHARD SIMMONS. I am a carter and carrier - these coats were in my van, in my possession. On the 24th of February I was carrying them for Mr. Baker, and going up Queen-street - my man told me I was being robbed of some clothes; he said, "There is the man;" I saw the prisoner, followed him up Holborn, up Kingsgate-street and Eagle-street, and saw him drop the clothes - I took them up, and saw him taken.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Is the man who told you of it here? A. No; they had been in the bottom of the van; I saw them safe in Holborn about five minutes before.
JAMES JENNINGS. I was going through Eagle-street, and met the prisoner with two coats; I made way for him to pass - I then heard a cry of Stop thief! I pursued him; he dropped the coats not far from me - I followed him till he was taken.
JAMES BENSON. I am an officer. I was standing in my shop, and heard the alarm; I ran out, saw the prisoner running, followed him to Red Lion-square, and took him.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was walking up the street, and saw them; I picked them up; I was running, and heard Stop thief! called - I dropped them.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 27. - Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-249]
1052. THOMAS LAWRENCE and ROBERT TAYLOR were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of March, 1 stove, value 10s., the goods of John Povey; and that Lawrence had been before convicted of felony.

I was opposite Mr. Poney's shop, who is an ironmonger, on the 2nd of March, and saw the prisoners there; they each of them took hold of an end of this stove, and carried it off - I followed, and took them with it; I held them till the prosecutor and the officer came.
GEORGE HOBBY. I am a Police-constable. I took the prisoners into custody - this stove stood by their side.
JOHN POVEY. This stove is mine.
WILLIAM JAMIESON. I am a constable of St. Pancras. I have a certificate of the former conviction of Lawrence, by the name of John Lawrence; I was a witness, and know he is the man - (read.).
