1053. EDWARD OXBY was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 1 hat, value 18s., the goods of Samuel Gravenor.
SAMUEL WELLS, I know Mr. Gravenor's shop, in Bethnal-green-road. I saw the prisoner and another boy pass his door on the 28th of March, and the prisoner took a hat from the door; I called Stop thief! and pursued: some gentleman tried to stop him - he threw down the hat, and a gentleman brought it to me.
WILLIAM STRAY. I attend to the business of Mr. Samuel Gravenor. About eight o'clock in the evening I was informed there was a thief - I ran out, and saw the prisoner running with the hat - he threw it down; we ran on to Cabbage-court, where I lost sight of him for an instant, but a Policeman took him - I met him with him - I know he is the man.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that he tookthe hat intending to purchase it, but being rather inebriated it fell from his hand. He received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 21. - Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18320405-251]
1054. JOHN LEONARD PERRY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of February, 5 sacks of flour, value 15l., and 5 sacks, value 15s., the goods of Robert Jones; and JAMES CREAMER was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen.
ROBERT JONES. I am a baker, and live in Pollard's-row, Bethnal-green - I keep my flour in a loft, about a yard from my dwelling-house. It was perfectly safe at seven o'clock at night on the 16th of February - I cannot tell whether the loft door was locked, but we were repairing the oven, and I suppose those who took the flour most have got over the wall: my man came to my other house, and said we had lost five sacks of flour - I got up, went to the loft, and missed them; they were worth about 15l. - the sacks I do not pay for, we return them; I found the two prisoners in custody the next day - I cannot swear to the flour, but I can to the sacks; we have had a great many with the same mark.
JAMES GLIBBERY I am a Police-officer. On the 17th of February I saw Perry in Church-street, Bethnal-green, driving a horse and cart, containing five sacks of flour; I suspected it was not right, and watched him, with my brother officer - he went on to Creamer's house, and the cart stopped at his door; the shutters were up, but the door opened, and Creamer came out - they said something to each other; I then went and asked Creamer who the flour belonged to - he said to him; I said I must take him, and I gave him to my brother officer; Perry was then on the cart, preparing to unload; the cart belonged to somebody belonging to the fire-office - it was between six and seven o'clock in the morning.
Creamer. You took another man, and allowed him to escape. Witness. No - I took you directly; there was another man, who made his escape from my brother officer.
THOMAS CAMROUX. I was with Glibbery. I saw Perry driving the cart from Church-street to Fashion-street, where it stopped, at Creamer's; I stopped while Glibbery went to the station and got another officer - we then went and asked Creamer whose it was; he said it was his - there was another man in custody of the other officer, but he got away - I showed the same sacks and flour to the prosecutor: the flour was given up, but the sacks are here.
WILLIAM CLARK. I am a Police-constable. On the 17th of February, soon after seven o'clock in the morning, I was desired to go with Glibbery to Fashion-street - I saw the cart at the door, with the five sacks of flour in it: I took Creamer - another man came out of the house, and ran away in his shirt-sleeves.
JEREMIAH PAINE. I was going down Pollard's-row, about half-past six o'clock, and saw Perry driving a little chaise cart, with the flour in it; I walked alongside of him I suppose, five hundred yards; an elderly man followed the cart, and crossed the way - I cannot swear to him, but I can to Perry.
Perry's Defence (written). On the evening of the 16th of February, as I was standing in Cammomile-street, Bishopsgate, a respectable looking man came and asked if I was not the person who drove Mr. Smith's horse and cart; I told him I was; he then told me that he wanted me to remove a few goods for him on the following morning, at six o'clock, which I said I would do - he came the next morning, got into the cart with me, and told me to drive to Bethnal-green-road, which I did; when I came to Pollard's-row, he told me to drive down there, and stop under a loft window - he then, with two other men, who were in the loft, loaded my cart with five sacks out of the loft; he told me to follow him, which I did, till I was about half-way down Fashion-street, Spitalfields; he then told me to stop while he went over the way - he almost instantly returned, and said that the person the goods were for had removed, and I must wait while he went back to get his direction; while he was gone I got up on the cart to tie up one of the sacks, and three Policemen came up and asked me where the owner of the sacks was; I told them he was in the house opposite; they went in and brought him out, but let him make his escape.
Creamer's Defence. Two men came to inquire for a baker who had lodged with me; I said he was gone, but I could give them a direction where to find him - I was about doing so when the officer came and took the man who was waiting for the direction; they let him go, and then came and took me - the cart stood opposite my door; the baker had instructed me to give any one his direction who might call for it - I did not know what the sack contained; I did not come outside my own shop - the shutters were up; I did not say the flour was my property - I have the shop and parlour, and have no communication with the rest of the house.
PERRY - GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Seven Years.
CREAMER - GUILTY. Aged 48.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-252]
1055. MARY ROBERTS and ANN SLANEY were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of February, 1 gown, value 10s., the goods of Mary Hughes; and PHOEBE JUGGINS was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to be stolen.
MARY HUGHES. I am single, and keep a chandler shop in St. Ann-street. This gown hung in my yard on the 21st of February, to dry: I saw Roberts and Slaney come to the shop, and have some bread and dripping - I looked out after they had left, and the gown was then safe - I missed it about one o'clock.
LEONARD GEORGE NEEDES. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Bridge-road, Lambeth. I have a gown pawned by Juggins, in company with Slaney, on the 21st of February - I lent 4s. on it; she said it was for her mother.
CHARLES ROBERT BEDWELL. I am an officer. On the morning of the 21st of February I was on my beat -I saw Slaney and Roberts walking with a basket, but I do not know what they had - I went on the 22nd to Slaney's house, and said I wanted her; she said to me directly,"You must take the other girl" - I said, "What girl?" she said, "Roberts" - I went with her to Roberts, and took her; I found 1s. 5d. on her, but no duplicate.
GEORGE GRIFFITH HOWELS. On the 23rd of February I was on duty - Joggins came to me, to the watch-house, and said she believed she was wanted for stealing agown, which two girls had stolen two days before - I took her.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Slaney's Defence. A water-cress man gave it to me to pawn.
SLANEY - GUILTY. Aged 17.
JUGGINS - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
ROBERTS - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-253]
1056. JOHN ROACH was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of March, 1 watch, value 30s., and 1 watch-chain, value 1s., the goods of Edward Grubb, from his person.
EDWARD GRUBB. I am a labourer; I was walking with my wife in Lower Berner-street, Commercial-road, between eight and nine o'clock, in the evening of the 25th of March - just as I was turning a corner the prisoner came across the way, and snatched my watch; he ran off- I pursued, and did not lose sight of him till he was taken with it.
JOHN JEFFORD. I am an officer. I saw the prisoner running - he met me, and I stopped him; I tripped my foot against a scraper, and as I arose I saw him throw something from his hand; the prosecutor came up, and said he had taken his watch.
GEORGE ROBINS. I took up the watch.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was playing, and heard the cry of Stop thief! I ran, as other boys did - the officer stopped me, and the prosecutor said he had lost his watch, but he did not believe it was me, as there was a corner turned.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-254]
1057. STEPHEN SIBLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 2 shoes, value 5s.; 2 shirts, value 3s., and 1 duck frock, value 1s., the goods of William Wilson.
WILLIAM WILSON. I am a mariner. I lost the articles stated out of the cabin of my vessel, which lays in the river; the prisoner was on board - I told him I had missed them, but I have never seen them since.
JOHN REED. I am mate on board the ship. The prisoner was an apprentice on board - I know there was nothing lost out of the shop till he came on board; the prosecutor lost a pair of shoes, two shirts and a frock on the 22nd of March; I questioned the prisoner about them; he gave me no answer - I asked where he slept; he said at home; I said I would send to his father, which I did - he then absconded from the ship, and was not taken for same case; I then asked him what he had sold the shoes for, and he said half a crown.
JOHN LITTLE. I am a Thames Police-officer. I took the prisoner - I said he was charged with stealing Wilson's clothes; he said he had sold them to different Jews in Houndsditch - I went with him there, but he could not point them out.
Prisoner. This is the first time I ever stood at a bar; I hope you will have mercy.
GUILTY. - Aged 16. - Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18320405-255]
1058. JAMES STAMFORD was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 1 wheelbarrow, value 5s., the goods of Charles Evans.
JAMES POSFORD. I am a Police-constable. On the evening of the 30th of March I saw the prisoner and another man - the prisoner was wheeling the barrow down Tunbridge-street; I crossed the New-road, towards them; the prisoner let go the handles of the barrow, and ran away towards Battle-bridge - I knew him very well; I went after him, saw him come out of a penny show, and stopped him.
CHARLES EVANS. This barrow is mine, and was taken from my shop window at St. Pancras.
The prisoner received an excellent character.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-256]
Fourth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1059. MARY STEVENS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of February, 1 shirt, value 5s.; 1 blanket, value 3s.; 1 pillow, value 2s., and 1 pillow-case, value 6d., the goods of Ann Shaw.
ANN SHAW. I am a widow, and live in Laystall-street, Holborn. The prisoner was my lodger, and a very good lodger; she came as a married woman - I could not get my rent of them; I put a padlock on the door - Hoskins, a man she lived with, brought the key in an hour afterwards - I missed these articles stated, and found the duplicates of them in a corner of the room.
JOSEPH HUETSON. I was in the service of a pawnbroker in Gray's Inn-lane. I have a blanket, pawned by the prisoner on the 3rd of November.
JAMES STANLEY. I am servant to a pawnbroker, in North-place, Gray's Inn. I have a shirt, a pillow, and a pillow-case, which were pawned by the prisoner.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-257]
1060. WILLIAM SIMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 1 watch, value 50s.; 1 waistcoat, value 5s., and 1 pair of trousers, value 5s., the goods of John Webster.
JOHN WEBSTER. I live in Turner-square, Hoxton - the prisoner lodged twice with me; the last time was up to the 2nd of April, 1826. I lost a waistcoat, a pair of trousers, and a watch: I have never seen them since - I went after the prisoner, and found him in Westminster on the 25th of March last; he had broken a lamp in the course of the week, before he left me, and borrowed 8s. of my wife - I missed the property about half-past four o'clock in the morning; the prisoner had come into my room, and sat down, and warmed himself - I was in bed; he never went to bed at all that night - we expected he was going to pay my wife a sovereign, as he had promised.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Then the prisoner came into your room? A. Yes; my articles were in the top drawer - there were five other lodgers in the house.
MARY WEBSTER. I am the prosecutor's wife. On the 1st of April, 1826, the prisoner came home from half-past twelve to one o'clock at night; I opened the door, but told him not to come in till I had got to bed - but directly I got in he rushed into the room, said he was very cold, and begged of me to let him warm himself by the fire; I sat up in my bed, and begged him to take the candle and go to bed, but instead of that he turned it upside down in the candlestick, and begged me to let him sit a bit; he laid his head down, and pretended to be asleep - I was angry with him for putting out the candle: I have a double sel of man lodging there, who work at the gas-works - I still sat up in the bed, but dozed off to sleep - when I awokehe had put out the fire with a tea-kettle of water, and I heard him at the handles of my drawers; I asked who was there - he made no answer: I got out of bed, and put my hands nearly to him - he said, "Don't be frightened, mistress, it is me, I won't hurt you:" I said, "What business have you here?" he said he could not get out - I said, Nonsense; he then went out, and I heard him go up the first flight of stairs - I heard no more till a little before three o'clock, when I heard somebody go out - I did not get out of bed when I heard the prisoner at my drawers; I was on my knees at the foot of the bed, and when I felt him he laughed at me - I did not alarm my husband, as I did not know he had taken any thing; when I heard somebody go out I thought it was a man who worked at the gas, but no other man went out that morning.
Cross-examined. Q. If he were in the dark, feeling his way out, would he not be very likely to brush against the drawers? A. Yes, that was what I thought he did do - I locked the door when I heard him go out a little before three o'clock, but not when he went out of my room - I did not go to sleep after he went out; I made a complaint to the officer, but I did not go before a Magistrate till the prisoner was found, which was on the 26th of March last - he owed us some money; he had been out of work some time, and we kept him - he had promised to bring us a sovereign that night; I never said I would be revenged on him, nor that I would do for him.
COURT. Q. What was it you lost? A. A silver watch, a steel chain, a kersey mere striped waistcoat, a pair of brown duck trousers, some caps, and 2s. 5d. - I had not been out of the room from the time I saw the watch safe; I missed it before six o'clock, when my husband came in for some halfpence, and they were gone from off the table where they had been - I am sure no one had been in the room from the time I saw the watch safe till the prisoner came - I suppose he left the room by twenty minutes after two o'clock; I knew a man would go out a little before three, and I thought with one getting up I would lock both doors.
GEORGE CARTER. I am a Police-constable. I took the prisoner at his own lodging, in Wilson-street, Westminster, on the 20th of March: he said he had lodged at the prosecutor's house, but he had no knowledge of the robbery.
JOHN WEBSTER. I went to his lodging at Westminster - I knocked at the door and asked if William Simpson lodged there: some man called him from his dinner - I said to him, "Will, how are you?" he said, "I don't know you," and pushed the door against me - I seized him, and said' "You are my prisoner;" he was stronger than I, and he got me out - I called a Policeman, but he could not get in, and he sent for some more to break the door open, but before they came the landlady opened the door, and the Policeman and I went in - we sat in conversation, and the prisoner called me Jack, and I said to the Policeman, "Take notice of that, he did not know me before:" I had seen my watch ten minutes before twelve o'clock at night.
Cross-examined. Q. How are you employed? A. In selling pies, and what I can - I did not ask the prisoner about the 8s., which he had borrowed.
JURY to MARY WEBSTER. Q. Did the other man go out that morning? A. No - he used generally to go to attend cows, but did not that morning; he used to give another man 1s. to do his work on a Sunday morning sometimes.
THOMAS TANNER. I lodged eight years with the prosecutor. The prisoner lodged there - he did not come to bed that night, and he left in the morning; the prosecutor came, and inquired for Simpson - I said he had not been there; he made some complaint of a loss at that time - he then came again, and mentioned about a watch and trousers; I had seen the prosecutor with a watch the Sunday before - he generally kept it in the room they lived in; I generally went in there to have my victuals, and so did the other lodgers - the drawer was not always locked.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you awake all night? A. No, but I do not think it likely he could come into the room without my hearing him.
Prisoner. When I lodged with him I had been working at the gas-house - I was then employed to lay the gas round Greenwich-college; I said it was too far for me to come, and they said they could not expect it.
JOHN WEBSTER. He was out of work entirely till the last week, when he said he had got work; and he took victuals with him from me every morning, and some halfpence to get him some beer - I went to every place, within a mile or two of London, in search of him.
MR. CHURCHILL called
MARY REDDY. I am a shoe-binder, and live in Wilson-street, in the same house as the prisoner did. On Monday last I saw Mrs. Webster in conversation with two women; I heard her say, "I have dreamed these last two or three nights, that this man I have in custody will get off, and if he does, I will have my revenge, so help me God."
COURT. Q. Did not you go to her and say, "What a horrid woman you are?" A. No; I came down with Mrs. Simpson to help her carry her child; I did not hear Mrs. Webster say any thing more; I went to Mrs. Simpson, and told her - I did not hear a single word beside what I have stated; she was speaking to two respectable women- one was oldish, and one youngish; I had never seen Mrs. Webster till I went to Queen-square, where I met with Mrs. Simpson - I am a shoe-binder; there are some men lodge in the house up stairs - I lodge in the front room, and have done so for nine weeks.
ANN BRADSHAW. I am married. The prisoner lodged with me at No. 22, Wilson-street, for two years - the prosecutor came there on the Sunday, and I objected to his coming in.
COURT. Q. Do you recollect an inquiry being made about him? A. Yes, on that Sunday one of our men came in and said, "Here is a man wants Simpson;" Simpson went to the door, and said, "Ah, Jack, how do you do?" the prosecutor said, "Get your hat and go along with me;" he said, "No, I am having my dinner" - he then seized him, and said, "You are my prisoner;" he was quite drunk when he came - he went and brought the officer in a quarter of an hour; the officer said, "Open the door;" I said, "I shall not;" he opened the window, and said,"How foolish you are" - I said, "I will not open the door to day;" he said, "You must."
GEORGE CARTER. When the door was opened, it was by the prisoner; the prosecutor was there - the prisonersaid, he knew nothing of him; I said, "You must come with me" - he said, "I will not;" I knocked several times, and this woman said she would not open the door; I spoke to her, and after some time she opened it - the prosecutor was quite sober, though he was agitated.
JURY. Q. Did you go there twice? A. No, only once; the prisoner said he did not know the prosecutor, and shut the door; the prosecutor said, "You know me well, Simpson;" the prisoner then banged the door too, and I opened the window.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-258]
1061. MARY BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of January, 8 shillings, the monies of William Blackburn.
CHARLES HORATIO AUGUSTUS KIRBY. On the 3rd of January the prisoner came to Mr. William Blackburn's, where I was shopman; she offered a pair of trousers in pawn, which I agreed to take for 8s. - she asked me to let her do them up herself; I did, and instead of giving me the trousers, she gave me these old breeches, not worth a farthing, pinned up in this wrapper, and I gave her the 8s. on them - I should not have given it her without I had supposed I had the trousers, which were worth the money I agreed to lend.
JOHN MAYERS. I am an officer. I took the prisoner on the 17th of February.
Prisoner's Defence. I never had any breeches in my possession in my life.
MR. KIRBY. I am certain she is the woman; she was a regular customer at our house.
GUILTY. Aged 42.
[trialAccount t18320405-259]
1062. MARY BROWN was again indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of February, 14 shillings, the monies of William Blackburn.
CHARLES HORATIO AUGUSTUS KIRBY. On the 3rd of February the prisoner brought a coat to Mr. Blackburn's, which she asked 14s. on; I looked at it, and agreed to lend it her - she asked to wrap it up herself, which I allowed her to do, and she left an old worthless blue coat(the other was an olive one), and I gave her the money; there were some shillings among it.
JOHN CHRISTOPHER HEWITT. I saw Mr. Kirby examine the olive coat on the evening of the 3rd of February; the prisoner asked a price above what he offered her - after a cavil, she agreed to take it; she then asked whether he would allow her to wrap up the coat herself, as her husband was very particular; he gave her the duplicate and the money, and she left this coat.
GUILTY, believing the prosecutor did not intend to part with his 14s. without having the olive coat. Aged 42.
[trialAccount t18320405-260]
1063. MARY BROWN was again indicted for stealing, on the 7th of December, 18 shillings, the monies of William Blackburn.
CHARLES HORATIO AUGUSTUS KIRBY. I was shopman to Mr. William Blackburn; the money in each case was his. On the 7th of December, the prisoner came and offered me an olive coat for 18s., which I agreed to lend her; she wished to pin it up herself, and I gave it her for that purpose; while I was writing the duplicate she shifted the parcel, and left this worthless black coat, not worth 1s.; I had agreed to advance the money at the time, and I did advance it - I gave the coat to her merely for the purpose of pinning it up; I would not have advanced the money, without I supposed she had left the olive coat - I did not intend to part with the possession, or my claim on that coat.
Prisoner. The coat I pawned that night, I redeemed in less than an hour. Witness. There was one night she redeemed a coat, and brought it back in less than five minutes, and this may be the coat; I cannot say that she did not redeem this one afterwards; she did not take a coat out that night.
GUILTY. Aged 42. - Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18320405-261]
1064. JOHN WILKINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of February, 1 pair of shoes, value 12s., the goods of William Worster.
SECOND COUNT, stating it to be the goods of William Rockingham.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM ROCKINGHAM. I am captain of a fly-boat, belonging to Messrs. Worster and Co.; it came to London. on the 1st of February. When we were at Buck by wharf, a brown-paper parcel was put on board by Mr. William Worster - it was placed under the tarpauling, and well secured down; I employed the prisoner to assist us from Paddington, through Islington-tunnel, to the warehouse in the City-road; I assisted in unloading the vessel, and missed the parcel in about an hour after we began to unload - on the 17th of February I received information, in consequence of which I saw the prisoner at the British Lion in Brick-lane, City-road - I said I understood he knew something about a parcel I had lost; he said he did not - I said, "If you do, and will produce the duplicate, I will be much obliged to you;" he then said, "If you will go to the Windsor Castle I will bring it you in an hour;" he said he knew it was pawned for 4s. - he went away, and we saw him no more till he was taken.
Cross-examined by MR. DONNE. Q. Where did you take the prisoner on board? A. I was in bed at the time he came on board - here is a man who was at work when he came; I took him to do my work while I was asleep- I had engaged him on the Friday night before; he came on the 3rd of February; I did not see him on board till we got to the wharf at the City-road.
COURT. Q. Is it a navigable canal? A. Yes; it came from Market Harborough to London.
THOMAS ROCKINGHAM. I was a boatman on board the boat. I remember the parcel coming on board; I do not know what was in it; the prisoner came on board, about five or six miles from Paddington, about eleven or twelve o'clock at night - he came with me up to the basin, in the City-road.
Cross-examined. Q. What night did he come on board? A. On the 3rd of February, five or six miles on the other side of Paddington; he stepped on board, but at no particular place.
WILLIAM WADSWORTH. I live at Long Buckby. I made this pair of shoes for Mr. William Worster and Co.
Cross-examined. Q. Who did you give them to? A. To Miss Worster.
MISS ELIZABETH WORSTER. I received the pair of shoes from him, and gave them to Joseph Lee to take to the wharf.
JOSEPH LEE. This lady gave me the parcel - I took it to the wharf, and delivered it to Mr. Bland.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there any direction on it? A. I did not see any.
THOMAS BLAND. I received a pair of shoes in a parcel from Lee - I directed them to "Mr. William Worster, City-road, London," and delivered them to Shaw to put on board the boat.
JAMES SHAW. I received the parcel, and gave it to Thomas Rockingham.
Cross-examined. Q. You cannot say what was in the parcel? A. I cannot say, but from the feel of it I believe it was shoes; it was directed to Mr. William Worster, the son of one of our partners, in London.
WILLIAM WEBB. I am servant to Mr. Joseph Cooper; they call me by the nick-name of Oxford. On Friday, the 10th of February, I was at the British Lion - the prisoner came there, and said he had a ticket of a pair of shoes; he let me look at it - he said they were in pawn for 4s.; I was to see if they would fit me - I was to give him 1s. 6d.; he said he had them made by Barker, in Macclesfield-street, and gave 11s. for them; I did not go for them - I gave up the ticket to Noah Elwell.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you ask the prisoner any questions? A. No - I did not see the shoes.
NOAH ELWELL. I am captain of a boat, in the service of Messrs Worster. On the 17th of February I was in the tap-room of the Windsor Castle - I saw the prisoner there that morning; he asked me where Rockingham was - I said on board; he said he had been giving him a pretty character - I said, "Great cause he had;" he said it was about producing a parcel; I said, "If he don't produce that parcel he will be discharged, and his men and all;" he said if they were in pawn he could get the ticket - I told Rockingham of it; I went with him the same day to a pawnbroker's, near St. Luke's church, and got the shoes there; Mr. Holt went, and saw the shoes, but he did not take them - we got them by means of the ticket, which we got from Webb.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you work in that boat? A. No; he works one boat, and I another - we went to the pawnbroker's, and inquired for a pair of cork-bottomed shoes, pawned on Saturday or Friday for 4s.; I did not mention the prisoner's name or any other.
THOMAS CASSIDY (Police-constable N 71.) On the 21st of February I apprehended the prisoner, and received the duplicate from Holt.
GEORGE HOLT. I received the duplicate from Elwell, and gave it to the officer.
CHARLES WORLEY. I am foreman to Mr. Cassel, a pawnbroker. I had the shoes; they were pawned with me by a bargeman, but I do not remember his face; I gave this duplicate of them.
Cross-examined. Q. What do you mean by a bargeman? A. A man in the dress of a bargeman; he gave his name as Thomas Cocktail, and said he worked at Pickford's wharf; the men frequently live on board the barges - it was on the 3rd of February, and I think in the morning - it was day-light.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. I believe, when you speak of daylight, it was as much light as comes into a little box? A. We have plenty of light in our shop - nothing is more common than for persons to pawn in a name that is not their own.
MR. DONNE to THOMAS ROCKINGHAM. Q. How many persons were on board the boat when it came to the City basin? A. Four, altogether - the prisoner, another man, my brother, who was in bed, and myself; the other man who was on board, was dressed like a bargeman.
GUILTY. Aged 25. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-262]
1065. ELIZA BALDWINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 gown, value 14s., the goods of Daniel Brice.
DANIEL BRICE. I live in Brownlow-street, Drury-lane, and am an organ-builder. The prisoner lodged with me for about four months; she left on Saturday, the 26th of March - I then missed a long train dress from a wardrobe - there are working persons who reside in the house; the prisoner represented herself as a servant out of a situation - she had access to the front parlour, in which the wardrobe was; my wife and child sleep there.
ROBERT HARRIS. I am shopman to Mr. Newberry, a pawnbroker, in Drury-lane. I have a black velvet dress, pawned by the prisoner, on the 23rd of March, in the name of Ann Bennett, No. 6, Brownlow-street; the prosecutor lives at No. 8; I think I had seen the prisoner before, and I saw her two or three days afterwards.
DANIEL BRICE. This is my dress - I do not know what rent the prisoner paid; I think I was out of town when she took the lodging, but her money being spent, and having a dissipated mother-in-law, and but few clothes, I said she might stay and do needle-work - she left me on the Saturday, to go to a place in Oxford-street, as she told us - on the Monday we missed a gown, and on further search we missed this dress; her mother is a kind of charwoman at the Olympic theatre, and her father is a servant there.
ROBERT SUTTLE (Police-constable B 97.) I took the prisoner.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-263]
1066. JOHN BARRETT was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of March, 12 ozs. of soap, value 6d.; 2 knives, value 2s.; 1 fork, value 1s., and 1 3/4 lb. of tea, value 10s., the goods of Thomas Woodward, his master.
THOMAS WOODWARD. I am a grocer, and live in Piccadilly - the prisoner was my porter for some weeks. On the 7th of March, about seven o'clock, I found a piece of soap on him - he had hidden it, and my young man found it, and marked it with the letters T. N. - I found it on him, with the mark; I sent for the officer, and gave him in charge - I went to his lodging, but did not go up stairs - I missed some knives and forks, and some tea and other things.
THOMAS FARRANT (Police-constable C 42.) I received the prisoner at the watch-house; I went to the house, where I saw his mother - he had directed me there; I there got these two knives, this fork, and 1 lb. 11 ozs. of black and green tea; I mentioned it to the prisoner the next morning, and he said he had taken the knives and fork,but not the tea; I said the bag of tea was marked, and he said that was since I took it.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. My master used to beat me with a broom, and broke it in three pieces about my arms.
THOMAS WOODWARD. He was endeavouring to run away from me, and I got a broom-stick and hit him three times, to prevent it - I had a good character with him.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18320405-264]

[trialAccount t18320405-266]
1069. MARGARET SHELLY was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of March, 3 shillings, the monies of James Harris.
JAMES HARRIS. I am a dairyman, and live in Holland-street, Soho. The prisoner was my weekly servant, and carried out milk for me; I missed money from time to time, and got Murless, on the 5th of March, to mark a sovereign, five half-sovereigns, six shillings, and six sixpences - I put the money into a drawer in the desk in the parlour, and in about an hour I told the prisoner she had been robbing me - she said she had not; I sent for an officer, and three marked shillings were found on her.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you lock the desk? A. I did, and put the key into my pocket - I owe her 21s.; I always gave her money when she asked for it; the desk was not broken open - I paid none of the money away which Murless marked; I never said I suspected my sister.
JOHN HARWOOD. I am an officer. I searched the prisoner, and found one marked shilling in her mouth, and two in her stocking, under her foot; no key was found on her.
BARNARD MURLESS. I marked the money, and saw it put into the desk; these are three of the shillings that I marked.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-267]
1070. FRANCES HUBBARD was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 5 cups, value 5s.; 1 shirt, value 6d.; 1 penknife, value 6d.; 1 shirt-pin, value 1s., and 6 yards of ribbon, value 3s., the goods of William Wale, her master.
FRANCES WALE. I am the wife of William Wale - we keep the Bayswater tavern ; the prisoner was about fifteen months in my service. On Saturday, the 18th of February, suspicion arose about some tea that was found, and on the Monday morning Mr. Wale sent for the officer, who searched the prisoner's box, and found some baby's caps, and the other things - the knife was in her pocket.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I believe the prisoner was very much in your confidence? A. I put every trust in her; she usually sits in the bar-parlour with me, as she attends the bar - I have played at cards with her - I am fond of spirits; we all know our faults; I do not get tipsy - I am not compelled to tell what quantity I take; I never accused my husband of putting these things into the prisoner's box; I never said to him,"You wretch, you put these things in the girl's box" - she frequently sat up all night with me when we had heavy dinners; I have slept in the same room as her; I never stuck my husband in the hand out of jealousy of the prisoner - she did not give me warning to quit; I never said I would hang her if I could - I had a great deal of plate which she could have taken.
THOMAS TRINGHAM. I am an officer. On the 20th of February I took the prisoner, and requested to see her box; she unlocked it, and I found in it five child's caps, a child's shirt, and other things.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-268]
Before Mr. Recorder.
1071. SAMUEL RALF was indicted for stealing on the 1st of April, 2 sovereigns and 6 half-crowns, the monies of William German, from his person.
WILLIAM GERMAN. I am servant to Mr. Ranson, a baker, in the Minories. On the 1st of April, between seven and eight o'clock, I went into a public-house at the corner of Crispin-street, Spitalfields, and had in my left-hand waistcoat pocket two sovereigns and six half-crowns- I was sitting in a box with my head on the table, and fell asleep; I was not intoxicated - the prisoner, who I have known four years, went with me to the house; he was standing up when I fell asleep; he was close to the fire, not within reach of my pocket - I was awoke, and directly missed my money; he was then gone; I saw him in custody in about twenty minutes - he said he had got plenty of money, and produced two sovereigns, three half-crowns, a penny and a farthing, which he said were his own, and he could produce 10l. if it was wanted - he told me that morning that he had no money - he borrowed 6d. of me, and gave a duplicate as security for it.
JOHN MADGE. I am a baker, and live in Petticoat-lane - I know the prosecutor and prisoner. I saw them both at the public-house; I went in after them; the prosecutor laid asleep, with his head on the table - the prisoner was standing by the fire, smoking his pipe, about two or three feet from German; nobody else was in the room; I did not see the prisoner do any thing - he left three or four minutes after I came in; we both left together - I returned directly, having heard something at the bar, and awoke the prosecutor, who complained of losing his money in about five minutes - the prisoner was out of work then, and had been so for some time.
HANNAH HARNETT. I am servant at the public-house. The prosecutor and prisoner came in together; there was nobody else in the room; the prosecutor would not take any thing, but laid his head on the table, and went to sleep - the prisoner called for a pint of ale, and sat down by him; in about five minutes Madge came in;they both went outside the door; I saw the prisoner put his hand into the prosecutor's left-hand waistcoat pocket twice, and the second time I told master of it.
WILLIAM SAVAGE. I am a Policeman. I took the prisoner in charge; he produced two sovereigns, three half-crowns, a penny, and a halfpenny, and said he could prove where he had changed a sovereign that morning - before I heard of the robbery I saw him running up Red Lion-street; that was at a quarter to eight o'clock; the prosecutor informed me of the robbery - I went and found him.
JOHN BURROUGH. I am a constable. I went with Savage, and took the prisoner in charge.
Prisoner's Defence. I went into a coffee-shop - the prosecutor came in, and six or seven females in his company; the prosecutor took me to the public-house to have some gin - we had three or four half-pints, and five or six pints of ale; we returned to the coffee-shop, and then went to this house; he fell asleep - I went out, and talked to Madge at the door for two or three minutes; they knew I had money.
JOHN MADGE. I have not known him possessed of money lately.
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18320405-269]
1072. WILLIAM SWANEY was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 5 lbs. of bacon, value 1s. 8d., the goods of Charles Buckland.
MARY ANN WILDEN. I am related to Charles Buckland, who keeps a cheesemonger's-shop in Whitecross-street. I saw the prisoner take this bacon from the window, which was open - he was stopped with it.
HENRY CALDECOT. I live in Blue Anchor-alley. I saw the prisoner put his hand into the window, and run up Gloucester-court with the bacon.
GEORGE DOSSON. I am a Policeman. I stopped the prisoner with the bacon - I had seen him take it.
The prisoner pleaded poverty.
GUILTY. Aged 38. - Fined 1s. and Discharged.

1073. JOHN SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of February, 5 lbs. of beef, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Richard Procter.
CHARLES HERCOCK. I am servant to Richard Procter, a butcher. Between four and five o'clock in the afternoon I saw the prisoner take this beef from the board in front of the shop; I followed and stopped him, without losing sight of him - he was searched and no money found on him.
THOMAS RAYLE. I am a Policeman. I saw the prisoner going from the shop, and received him in charge - he had also got a loin of pork, and part of a leg of mutton; and two pieces of beef in his hat, and another in his pocket, a dried haddock and a newspaper of the day - he did not say where he got them.

1074. THOMAS SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 1 handkerchief, value 4d., and 1 apron, value 1s., the goods of Samuel Foreman.
SAMUEL FOREMAN. I live at Tottenham-mills. These things hung out to dry in my master's enclosed meadow; I stopped the prisoner with them in his hands, about one o'clock in the day, within two hundred yards of the place.
ELIZABETH FOREMAN. I am the prosecutor's wife. I hung these things to dry - I saw the prisoner about one o'clock, looking about the place, and in about ten minutes missed them.
CHARLOTTE FOREMAN. I watched the linen, and saw the prisoner take it off the hedge - my father followed and took him.
JAMES GRIFFITH. I received him in charge, and found 5d. on him - he said he was very sorry for it; I have known him three or four years, and know nothing wrong of him.
The prisoner pleaded poverty.
GUILTY. Aged 65. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18320405-272]
1075. SAMUEL SWINEY was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 silver tea-spoon, value 3s., the goods of Jane White.
ELIZABETH WHITE. I am housemaid to Mrs. Jane White, of Euston-place, New-road. I saw the prisoner by our kitchen door, and asked what he came for; he asked if I wanted to buy lemons - he had three in a basket; I asked why he came down the area - he said he was told to come down; I said I was certain he was not - Mrs. White, who heard me, came down; I missed the spoon, which I had seen safe ten minutes before - he was then talking to Mrs. White at the area gate; I said he had got the spoon - he immediately threw it down, saying, "That is all I have," and ran away; I went out and he was brought back - I said, "You have got a silver spoon;" he said, "I have not got it, but will show you where it is;" he picked it up in the area, and said, "Here it is, that is all I have taken."
Prisoner. Q. Was the kitchen door open or shut? A. Open when I saw you.
JOHN BIGMORE. I am a clerk. I was in the New-road; I heard a lady and White call out Stop thief! I saw the prisoner drop a basket, containing three lemons, and run across the road - I followed, and brought him back; he showed me the prosecutrix's house; White came out, and said, "You have got a spoon;" he said,"I have not got it, but will show you where it is;" he took it up in the area, and said it was through distress he had done it.
JOSEPH GREEN. I am a Policeman. I took him in charge, and asked why he went down in the kitchen; he said he did not know.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was about knocking at the prosecutrix's door; a man came up and said, "If you want to sell your lemons, you must go down stairs;" I stood at the kitchen-door, and knocked - the wind blew it open; the witness asked where my licence was, and threatened to give me in charge, and I ran away, fearing to be given in charge for not having a licence.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-273]
1067. WILLIAM SINCLAIR was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of March, 1 ham, value 12s., the goods of Charles James Webb.
ROBERT SARGENT. I am a shopman to Charles James Webb, cheesemonger, of Oxford-street. The prisoner came and bought a piece of bacon, which he paid for, and walked out; he then walked to and fro in front of the window, and while I turned my head to serve a customer, our boy called out that a ham was taken; I instantly ran out - the Policeman had taken him about fourteen yards from the shop.
WILLIAM OSBORN. I am a Policeman. I saw the prisoner take the ham from the window, and put it down; he then went and took it up; I took him with it - it was about seven o'clock in the evening.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I do not recollect meddling with the ham; I certainly laid hold of the knuckle.
GUILTY. Aged 44. - Confined Fourteen Days.

1077. JOHN DOWNES was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 2 sovereigns, the monies of James Smith.
SECOND COUNT, stating them to belong to Richard Chambers.
JAMES SMITH. I live in Black Boy-yard, Whitechapel, and am a seaman - this sovereign was in a box of my sister's, which was in her bed-room; Richard Chambers is her husband - the prisoner worked in the room above, as a slipper-maker; the sovereign was missed on the 30th of March - the prisoner was absent from then till the 3rd of April, when he gave himself up.
MARY CHAMBERS. I am the wife of Richard Chambers. My brother gave me three sovereigns on Friday, the 30th of March; I put them into a box, which was not locked, nor was the room-door - the prisoner left between seven and eight o'clock that evening; I missed two sovereigns next morning - he came to the house last Tuesday week, and said he had taken the money and spent it - he was given in charge.
JOHN RANSOM. I received him in charge - he said he had lost some of the money, and spent some.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Confined Three Months.

1078. MARY DONOVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 1 blanket, value 2s. 6d.; 1 shirt, value 2s.; 1 petticoat, value 8d., and 1 apron, value 9d., the goods of John Orman.
JOHN ORMAN. I am a silk-weaver, and live in White Horse-court, Spitalfields. The prisoner had been in the workhouse with me and my family - we had a fever, and were turned out of the workhouse; my wife gave her leave to sit up a night or two in our place, as she had no where to sleep - this continued about five weeks, and on the 2nd of April, I went to try to get a shilling from the overseers; I came home about seven o'clock in the evening, and she appeared to have been drinking; she said she was going to the top of the court, and would be back in a few minutes - on putting down my bed to make it, I missed this blanket, and then the other things; she was taken the same night, and the brush found on her.
SAMUEL HAYES. I am a pawnbroker. I have a blanket, pawned on the 2nd of April, in the name of Donovan, but not by the prisoner.
ARTHUR SOWERBY. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Wheeler-street. I have a child's petticoat and shirt, and two pillows, pawned at different times - I cannot swear who by.
RICHARD HUNT. I am a constable. I took the prisoner, and found the duplicates of this property on her - she had two aprons on, and a brush in her pocket.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I intended to redeem them at night, but the pawnbrokers were shut up when I went.
GUILTY. Aged 35. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-276]
1079. MARY DRISCOL was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 1 table-spoon, value 8s., and 1 handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of Thomas Duncan, her master.
PENELOPE DUNCAN. I am the wife of Thomas Duncan- we live in Shakespeare's-walk, Shadwell - the prisoner was in our service about five weeks, but did not sleep in the house, as she was in want of clothing. On the 17th of March, in consequence of suspicion, I told my son to watch her when she went home, and on Sunday morning, the 18th, I missed a table-spoon and handkerchief, which were safe at two o'clock the day before - I kept it quiet, and on Monday morning, went to Austin's, the pawnbroker's, and found my table-spoon; I did not send her to pawn it- I lost six handkerchiefs.
SAMUEL WILLIAMS. I am shopman to Mr. Austin. The prisoner pawned this table-spoon, between nine and ten o'clock at night, on the 17th of March, for 5s. in Mrs. Duncan's name - I saw her in custody on the 19th, and was quite certain of her.
HENRY WELLS. I am in the employ of Mr. Kennedy, pawnbroker, of Shadwell. I have a handkerchief pawned on the 29th of February, for 2s., in the name of Ann Driscol, by the prisoner.
Prisoner. She has lodgers who might take them.
PENELOPE DUNCAN. An old lady and her husband lodge in the house. I have known them forty years; it is an old fashioned house, and is divided - no other lodgers go up my stairs.
Prisoner. She suspected Mrs. Wood. Witness. I did so, because the prisoner told me Wood was taking my coals.(Property produced and sworn to.)
HENRY LEE. I am a Policeman. I took the prisoner in charge, and asked her at the station-house what she had done with the duplicates; she said she had not got the duplicates, but she took the spoon.
Prisoner. I did not say I took it; my mistress said if I would confess it, she would let me free - I said, I could not own to a thing I did not take. Witness. I am sure she did.
GUILTY. Aged 15. - Transported for Seven Years.

1080. ANN EDMONDS was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 shirt, value 3s., the goods of William Daniels.
JOHN DAWSON. I am shopman to William Daniel, who is a pawnbroker. This shirt hung in the shop for sale; I saw it about eight o'clock in the evening of the 28th of March - I missed it about eleven o'clock the next morning - I do not remember seeing the prisoner.
CHARLES HENRY WATERS. I am apprentice to Mr. Gofton, pawnbroker, of Gilbert-street, Oxford-street. The prisoner came to our shop to pawn a frock; I saw this shirt in her possession, and in consequence of suspicion,stopped her, and gave her into custody - she said she had it from a young man in Marylebone-lane, and was going to take it to his mother to wash.
WILLIAM TRODD. I am a Policeman. I received the prisoner in charge; she did not tell me she had the shirt from Marylebone-lane - she ran away as I took her to the watch-house, but I caught her.(Property produced and sworn to.)

1081. GEORGE FREEMAN and JOSEPH CALLAGHAN were indicted for stealing, on the 31st of March, 1 pair of boots, value 3s. 6d., the goods of Moses Packer.
MOSES PACKER. I live in High-street, Shadwell, and am a shoemaker. These boots hung inside my door; I saw them safe about nine o'clock - the prisoners were taken directly after; I had not seen them near the shop - the boots have not been found.
LYDIA BLUNT. I am fourteen years old. I came by the prosecutor's shop about nine o'clock in the evening, and saw the prisoner Callaghan go in and take the boots from inside the shop; they were both in company - Freeman remained outside; they went away together - I followed them, but could not see which way they went; I saw them in custody in about a quarter of an hour - I live in Dock-street; my father is a shoemaker - they both ran away together; Freeman said he would hit me if I did not go away.
JOHN HENHAM. I am a Policeman. I apprehended the prisoners about nine o'clock that night, both in company; Callaghan denied taking the boots, but said another boy had got them - I had information, and took them within ten minutes of the robbery; I found five dots on each of their hands, which signifies that they belong to the Forty Thieves.
FREEMAN - GUILTY. Aged 15.
CALLAGHAN - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-279]
1082. ANTHONY LEWORTHY GARLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 13 balls of string, value 12s.; 8 lbs. of hemp, value 9s.; 4 brushes, value 2l., and 3 gross of camel hair pencils, value 9s., the goods of William Thompson, his master.
WILLIAM THOMPSON. I am a brush-maker, and live in Gray's Inn-road. The prisoner was my journeyman for about ten weeks - I had information, in consequence of which I examined my stock, and missed this string and a quantity of other things; I had missed camel hair pencils before - the four brushes were worth more than 2l.; I had the prisoner apprehended on the 2nd of April - I am certain the articles in question are part of my stock, and could never have been sold - the brushes are not finished.
JAMES MANDELL. I am a Policeman. On Monday, the 2nd of April, about five o'clock in the afternoon the prisoner was called out of a public-house in St. John-street - I took him, and said he was charged with robbing his employer; he said, "Very well, I will go with you;" he did not deny it - I found on him eleven duplicates; one was for some brushes, and one for three gross of camel hair pencils - nine were for wearing-apparel.
JOHN PERRIN. I am a greengrocer. The prisoner lodged with me nearly twelve months - I intimated to Mr. Thompson suspicions which I had - I saw six brushes of this description in the prisoner's cupboard since Christmas, but cannot say when; and he brought some string home.
JOSEPH GILKES. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Gray's lon-road; I have known the prisoner some time by sight. On Thursday evening, the 29th of March, he brought me a ball of twine, as a sample of some, and offered it for sale; he said he had more, and that he took it in barter for some things, at 1s. 6d. a pound; he then offered me some at 14d. - I asked why he sold it at 4d. a pound less than it cost him; he said he had bartered it for other goods that he must have lost considerably by, and that he lived at Perrin's, in Charlotte-street - he went home, and brought ten more bundles, they weighed 5 1/2lbs.; I bought them of him - he had 8d. in money, and the rest in goods - on Monday, the 2nd of April, about half-past ten o'clock, Perrin came to me; I told him of this, in consequence of what he stated, and took the twine to Mr. Thompson, who said he could swear to it.
ALEXANDER GREEN. I am a harness-maker. I bought 8 lbs. of hemp of the prisoner, about three weeks before he was taken; I afterwards took it to Thompson's son, who claimed it, and compared it with some in his warehouse -I bought two balls of twine of him, on the 29th of March, for 10d.; Thompson claimed that.
JOHN ABETHELL. I am servant to Mr. Loveday, a pawnbroker. On the 28th of January, the prisoner pawned four brushes, in the name of Anthony Garland, No. 22, Charlotte-street; I knew him before.
JAMES MOTT. I am apprentice to Mr. Bulworthy, pawnbroker. On the 15th of February, a man, not the prisoner, pawned some brushes, in the name of Charles Lilly, No. 3, Clerkenwell-green; they are very small, and I put them down as brushes.
CHARLES LILLY. I am a watch-guard and chain maker. On the 15th of February I pawned three gross of camel-hair pencils for him, for 5s.; I live at Clerkenwellgreen - I gave him the duplicate; this is it - Thompson swore to a mark on the paper the pencils are in.
Prisoner. Q. Where they not pawned to advance your brother money to get his tools out of pawn? A. I do not know; you lent him 3s. - you said you had been in business at Taunton, and produced these things as your old stock.
JAMES MANDELL. I found on him this duplicate in the name of Lilly, and one in the name of Garland.
MR. THOMPSON. The writing on the paper these camel hair parcels are in, is the writing of one of my men - this string is a very peculiar sort; I have not the smallest doubt of its being part of my stock - I missed a great deal more; these four brushes, I am confident, are mine - I always get up this description of work myself; they have never been sold - the hemp has the same mark on the paper as is on my stock; I miss a great deal.
Prisoner. The writing on the paper the pencils are in, is my own. Witness. I am confident it is the writing of a servant I had in my employ; I know the paper the brushes are in; I know the long badger hair brushes from the manner of their manufacture - I never sold them, and cannot be mistaken in them; I could point them out among one thousand; and I never saw such twine as this in London.
Prisoner's Defence. I carried on business as a brush-maker in Somersetshire for several years, and was cryer to the Court of Quarter Sessions there, but from unforeseen circumstances, was obliged to give up business and my situation; the pencils are part of the stock I had left, and the paper the goods are in I bought of Mr. Hasty, opposite where I lodge - the writing on it is my own - the four brushes are my own manufacture; as to the string, I bought it of a hawker in the street, in exchange for other goods.
GUILTY. Aged 36. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-280]
1083. MARY HONER was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 1 gown, value 1s.; 2 petticoats, value 1s., and 1 shift, value 6d., the goods of Eleanor Moore.
ELEANOR MOORE. I live in Chamber-street, Whitechapel. On the 4th of April I was at dinner in the front kitchen; the prisoner came and looked into the kitchen; she was a stranger - I followed her, and asked her, on the top of the stairs, what she wanted; she said Nothing; I went down, and told master - he went up, and we found all these things of mine laying in the passage; she had them under her shawl - he ran out, and took her - she got from him; I followed into Rosemary-lane, and gave her in charge.
JOHN FARNES. I am a Policeman. I took her in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was taken ill, and seeing the door open, went in to look for a convenience.
GUILTY. Aged 52. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18320405-281]
1084. JOSEPH HUGHES was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of March, 1 bag, value 6d., and 19s. 10d. in copper money, the property of William Clay.
SECOND COUNT, stating it to be the property of George Tucker.
GEORGE TUCKER. I am carman to William Clay, saltmerchant, of Lower Thames-street. On the 4th of March, about five o'clock, I received four 5s. papers of copper from the soup-house in Brick-lane, and put them into a bag on a board, at the top of my cart - I rode in front; the cart was empty; I observed the prisoner following the cart, and kept looking behind now and then - he must have got into the cart to take it, and in Old-street I saw him in the cart; I came round, and called out,"Oh, you thief! you are there" - he had then got the bag of copper in his arms; he jumped out; I called Stop thief! he was stopped before I lost sight of him - he dropped the bag in the bed of the cart - I am sure he moved it from where I had placed it.
EDWARD HARDY. I am a weaver, and live at Hoxton. I saw Tucker riding in front of his cart, and when he called out I saw the prisoner in front of the cart, with the money in his arms - he jumped out, leaving it behind.
HENRY JAMES LARDNER. I am a gun-maker. I was in Old-street-road, and secured the prisoner.
GEORGE KEMP. I am a Policeman. I received him in charge - the bag contains 19s. 11d. in copper.
JAMES GLIBBERY. I am a sergeant of Police. When the prisoner was brought to the station, he said distress caused him to do it, and if he got through this he would not he in trouble again.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-282]
1085. ALFRED HOLT was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of February, 1 knife-case, value 18d., the goods of John Pistor, and that he had before been convicted of felony.
JOHN PISTOR. I am a broker, and live in Walbrook-place, Hoxton. On the 4th of February this knife-case was in the front part of the shop - I was absent about two hours, and did not miss it till the Policeman stopped the prisoner with it.
JOHN COLLINS. I am a Policeman. On the 24th of February, about half-past six o'clock, I stopped the prisoner with the knife-case under his arm, about two hundred yards from Pistor's shop; he said he had brought it from his sister's, at Islington.
GEORGE KEMP. I am a constable. I have a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction in this Court -(read); I know he is the man.
Prisoner. I get my living by selling things in the street - the Police have disturbed me several times, which forced me to commit this crime.
GUILTY. Aged 19. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-283]
1086. WILLIAM JONES and ELIZABETH WOOD were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of March, 14 shillings, the monies of John Snowsell, from his person.
JOHN SNOWSELL. I am a sawyer, and live at Harrow-on-the-Hill. On the 17th of March I was at Chelsea, at the committee meeting of a benefit society; I had 16s. 6d. in my pocket when I left the public-house - I was perfectly sober; it had just turned eleven o'clock when I left; I thought it too late to return to Harrow - I saw a young woman in the street, who directed me to a lodging in Royal Hospital-row - I had a bed-room there to myself; I saw neither of the prisoners till I got there - I went to bed about twenty minutes or half-past eleven o'clock; I did not lock or bolt the room door - the money was in my trousers pocket, under the pillow - when I had been in bed about a quarter of an hour, a man came into the room, and told me I could not stop there; I had paid a young woman 2s. 6d. for the use of the bed - I said I was very sorry, as I had paid for the bed; I said I should go if that was the case, and as I was putting on my stockings, the prisoner Jones came into the room - I had not seen him before; my trousers were then by my side; I laid hold of them to put them on - Jones caught hold of them; I laid hold of them to try to get them away; the prisoner Wood then came in, and pulled me away from him - the trousers were left in Jones' possession; they both went out on the landing-place, shutting the door after them, but in about a minute Wood threw my trousers into the room; my pocket was then cut off - neither of them said any thing; I waited in the room about half an hour with the man who had come in at first - he had not assisted in robbing me; Jones then came into the room again, and said, "What's the matter?" - the man said,"Why, he has lost his money;" Jones said, "It is a pity the man should be robbed, I will give him 2s.," but he did not - he asked if I would go to a public-house; but Isaid it was too late for that; the moment I got up to go out of the room, Jones ran away - I described them to the officer, who secured them in about an hour; I saw them both at the station, and I am sure of them both - a man came next morning, and gave me 8s. - I lost 14s.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Why did you take 8s. from a strange man? A. Because I had no money, and was obliged to return to Harrow, or lose my place; he called himself the prisoner's brother - it was given not to appear against them the next day; I told him I was obliged to go down to Harrow, and promised not to appear - I am married - the benefit society meet at the Green Dragon; they do not let beds there; I have no doubt the woman who directed me to the lodging was a prostitute; she took me to the room which she said she had to let - she had the key of the room - it is on a straight stair-case, leading to rooms, without any outer door; she lighted a candle, and left me; I did not put the candle out; I considered the first man who came in a respectable man - I was afraid to ask him to help me secure the prisoners; he said he was owner of the room; I said then I had been deceived - I had him taken to the watch-house, thinking as both ran away down stairs, they were both bad; I never said I could not swear to the man who took the money - a woman was present when I took the 8s. - that is her (looking at a woman named Graham): I did not say in her presence that I could not swear to the man - I have been a witness in this Court twenty or thirty times, or I may say fifty; I belonged to Bow-street at that time; I have been here once since I left Bow-street; Mr. Birnie discharged me from Bow-street, because it was my duty to be up all night at the office, and a gentleman came and asked my assistance in recovering his daughter who was going to be taken away - I left the office with him; Sir Richard was angry, and discharged me; the gentleman was a musician in Lord Liverpool's service -I was never prosecuted on any account; I could not tell that the woman I asked for a lodging was a prostitute - she was talking to another girl.
COURT. Q. Did any familiarities take place between you and her? A. None at all; I did not expect to see her again - Graham gave the man the 8s, which he gave to me; I took it as part of what I had lost.
Wood. He said he could not swear to me. Witness. I never said so, nor any thing of the kind; I did not say the girl who took the room sent the man up to rob me, nor that I had treated her with brandy-and-water.
Wood. I had been drinking with that woman all that day, and the prosecutor met her and gave her drink. Witness. The prisoner is not the girl who was with her.
HENRY WILLIAMS. I am a Policeman. I saw the prisoner Jones running away from the prosecutor about one o'clock at night; the prosecutor called out Police, Stop him! this was about fifty or sixty yards from the house - I ran across, and asked the prosecutor what was the matter; in consequence of what he said, I followed the prisoner up a court, and found another man there; he parted from Jones, and jumped over a wall; I laid hold of the other man first - I knew a house Jones frequented; I went there, knocked at the door, and the female prisoner's brother said, "Who is there?" I found Jones in the house, laying down by the door with his clothes on -Wood then flew out, and laid hold of me; she bit my knuckles - Jones then collared me; I sprang my rattle, and had a hard matter to secure him; my hand was bound up for a fortnight - this woman's name is not Graham, but Dockerty; the prosecutor charged the prisoners with this offence, and the man who jumped over the wall, with not assisting him to recover his money; the magistrate discharged him when Jones was committed; he told the prosecutor he would murder him - the prosecutor gave the same account at the watch-house as he has now.
Cross-examined. Q. Was Snowsell quite sober? A. He was; the house he was robbed at is not a notorious house; there are some respectable people lodge in it, and about two women of the town; I never knew any body go into that room - there may be twenty rooms in the house; I have not been looking for the woman the prosecutor met in the street; I know her, she is a prostitute, but does not walk the streets - she lives with a man they call her husband, and he is the man I took for ordering the prosecutor out of the room - I found a knife on that man, but no money.
Jones' Defence. I knew nothing of the robbery. Snowsell perjured himself on the trial of Richard Cummins, in 1825.
Wood's Defence. I am innocent.
JONES - GUILTY. Aged 20.
WOOD - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18320405-284]
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1087. RICHARD LOVELL and JAMES EVERETT were indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 1 live tame rabbit, price 7s.; 2 live tame fowls, price 5s.; 1 cap, value 6d., and 8 eggs, value 6d., the property of Thomas Chamberlain.
JOSEPH LEWIS (Policeman K 7.) On the 28th of March, about two o'clock in the morning, I heard a noise at the back of Providence-place, Bethnal-green - I got over a wall at the back of Chamberlain's house, and found a cap with eight eggs in it - I looked into the privy, and found the two prisoners there with a live tame rabbit and two fowls in a bag; I took them to the station - Chamberlain saw the property next morning, and claimed it.
THOMAS CHAMBERLAIN. I live at No. 8, Providence-place - I know nothing of the prisoners. I lost this property from my premises on the 28th of March, and found them at the station-house, with this rabbit and fowls, which are mine - the cap belongs to the premises; the hen was setting on nine eggs.
Lovell's Defence. I was at the play, and got over into these premises to sleep - I went into the privy; two men jumped over, we saw the eggs laying down, and put them into the cap.
LOVELL - GUILTY. Aged 16.
EVERETT - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320405-285]
1088. MARY LINDSEY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 3 blankets, value 6s.; 4 sheets, value 6s.; 1 pillow, value 1s., and 1 pillow-case, value 6d., the goods of Thomas Ward.
THOMAS WARD. I am a labourer, and live in Beaures-street, St. George's, Middlesex - the prisoner lodged seven weeks with me, in the name of Harris; a man came occasionally, after some time, who was described as her husband. The prisoner left on Monday, the 19th of March, and never returned - she took the key with her; I got into the room, and missed these articles; I afterwards found her, locked in a room, at a house in Duke-street.
WILLIAM OSMOND (Police-constable K 61.) I took the prisoner, and asked what she had done with the property; she said she had pawned them, but refused to tell where -I found thirty duplicates on her.
DANIEL KENNEDY. I am a pawnbroker, and live at Shadwell. I have a sheet, pawned with me by a woman on the 17th of March; this is the duplicate I gave for it.
JOHN ROSS. I am servant to Mr. Reynolds, a pawnbroker. I have a blanket and three sheets, pawned on different days - these are the duplicates given for them.
MARY WARD. I am the prosecutor's daughter. These articles were furnished to the prisoner, when she took the lodging.
Prisoner's Defence. I went for some money to redeem them, and should have redeemed them on Saturday -I gave the duplicates to the Policeman as soon as he took me.
GUILTY. Aged 29. - Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18320405-286]
1089. ELEANOR MILLER was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of February, 1 sheet, value 2s., the goods of John Foy.
THOMAS KEARNEY (Police-constable G 171.) On the night of the 25th of February the prisoner was given into my custody by the prosecutor - she owned she had taken the sheet to pawn, and given the duplicate to her husband.
Prisoner. I said I had wrapped the bit of a sheet round my shawl, which was in pawn - it was of no value itself. Witness. She said she had wrapped it round her own shawl.
JOHN FOY. I am a shoemaker, and have a house in Portpool-lane. The prisoner came with a man named Fitzgerald, and took a ready furnished room - she represented him as her husband; they lived together - I went into the room, and missed the sheet - I followed the man into the room, and gave him in charge; she acknowledged that she took it, and said the man was innocent, but the duplicate was found on him - I do not know that he was not her husband.
JOSEPH HUDSON. I am a pawnbroker. I have a sheet, pawned with this shawl, on the 21st of February.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor was quite tipsy: he brought me down stairs to look for my husband; he gave me a glass of gin, and gave me in charge - I left all my own things there.
JOHN FOY. She did not leave any thing in the room worth moving.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-287]

Fourth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1091. TUERESA REED was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of March, 1 table-cloth, value 1s. 6d.; 1 shift, value 1s.; 1 apron, value 6d.; 1 shawl, value 1s. 6d.; 1 handkerchief, value 6d.; 1 pair of stockings, value 6d.; 2 caps, value 2s.; 1 shirt, value 1s. 6d., and 1 basket, value 1s., the goods of Philip Fruchard.
MARY FRUCHARD. I am the wife of Philip Fruchard; he lives in Great New-street, Fetter-lane. On the 22nd of March I left the prisoner, who I knew before, at my house, about nine o'clock in the morning, when I went to my mother's; I returned at seven in the evening - she was then gone: I missed some Irish linen that night, and the next day I missed the shawl and these other articles.
Prisoner. Her daughter has my shawl, and wears it now - she had her own washed in the hospital, and I lent her mine to put on. Witness. The prisoner had been in Bartholomew-hospital, where my daughter is now dying; this shawl is my daughter's, but was at my place.
THOMAS GOLDING (Police-constable L 84.) I took the prisoner - she was wearing this shawl at the time, and had this basket in her hand, containing the tablecloth and other articles - she was then in Blackfriars, more than a mile from the prosecutrix's.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. She asked me to go and help her wash the next day - I saw her the night before, drinking with a man in a public-house, and that morning she said she had been out all night, drinking, and was not able to go to washing - I took the things to get washed by Sunday.
MRS. FRUCHARD. I did not ask her to help me - I was going to wash on the day I lost these things; she came to me that morning, and I asked her to have a cup of tea, because she was in distress - I had asked her the night before to call on me: I left her taking breakfast, while I went to my mother's, in Compton-street.
COURT. Q. Did you know where she lived? A. No; I had no conversation with her about washing - my daughter had not desired her to get them washed; they were in a pan where I keep dirty linen - I had been out to try to get my mother into the poorhouse; my husband was at home, and at work.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320405-289]
